I did not check FINDINGS.md before running it. A perfect brightness correction nearly DOUBLES the analytic score at every neighbour count, which is the largest single recoverable quantity found in the corruption. Two cautions before acting on it. The measure is brightness-sensitive by construction, and the learned matcher is already far more robust than it -- 0.316 against 0.085 -- so its marginal gain would be smaller. And an oracle is not a restorer: the per-fragment contrast and offset have to be estimated from a single 20x20 fragment carrying noise at sigma 40 to 55, which is the hard part. What this does establish is the target for the tile restorers, which are trained to reproduce the clean fragment and therefore already attempt exactly this: whether they succeed at the BRIGHTNESS part specifically has never been measured separately from their pixel loss

M278 | The corruption as a 2x2 of oracles: what levelling and denoising each buy, and together | KEY / MAPS THE REMAINING HEADROOM | scratchpad/two_oracles.py, 12 boards, analytic seam cost. The generator's chain run with each heavy stage switched off in turn; blur and JPEG stay on throughout since M276 measured them as mild | top-1 by neighbours known. Everything, which is what we are given: 0.085 / 0.167 / 0.291. Perfect levelling, no affine: 0.182 / 0.341 / 0.535. Perfect denoising, no noise: 0.156 / 0.204 / 0.328. BOTH, leaving only blur and JPEG: 0.502 / 0.713 / 0.853. Clean fragments: 0.813 / 0.931 / 0.975. Our learned matcher on the full corruption: 0.316 / 0.480 / 0.629 | the decomposition itself is a re-derivation of what `train_restore_tile.py` already documents (see M276); what is new here is the pairing. The two damages MULTIPLY rather than add -- each alone recovers to about 0.17 at one neighbour and together they reach 0.502, three times either. That is the shape of the problem: fixing one is worth little because the other still dominates. Two positions follow. Our learned matcher at 0.316 already beats either single ORACLE correction fed to the naive measure (0.182 and 0.156), so it is doing substantial work, and it sits between one oracle and two. And the reachable ceiling for a matcher on this data is somewhere above 0.502, since a learned measure beats the analytic one at every corruption level -- which lands suspiciously close to the "assembly threshold about 0.52" that `train_seam_embed.py` has carried in its docstring all along. The headroom is therefore real and roughly a factor of two, and it lives in restoration rather than in architecture. WITHDRAWN: the claim that these knobs are unused oversights is wrong. `--calibrate` was measured in M116, `--mix` in M65, `--ycc` in M62, `--target-mode noisefree` in M55; every one is off because it was tested and did not pay. `--restore-input` is the only one of the set with no prior entry

M279 | How much of the oracle ceiling the actual restorers recover | KEY / THE RESTORERS ARE THE UNDER-INVESTED NODE | scratchpad/restorer_value.py, 8 boards, the same analytic seam cost as M278 so the numbers share a scale. The tile restorers are trained to reproduce the clean fragment, so they attempt both of M278's corrections at once, which is the arm worth 0.502 | single-seam top-1: as given 0.076, restored by tile_restorer_mgc 0.101, the two restorers averaged 0.109, clean target 0.807. At four neighbours: 0.236, 0.326, 0.309, 0.986. M278's oracles for comparison: perfect levelling 0.182, perfect denoising 0.156, both 0.502 | the restorers recover 0.076 to 0.109, forty-three percent in relative terms and far below EITHER single oracle correction, let alone the 0.502 available from both. They are 0.4M-parameter networks trained for ten to twelve thousand steps, which is tiny beside the matchers they feed. Two consequences. `--restore-input` will give the matcher slightly better inputs than raw and therefore a modest gain, not the transformation M278's ceiling suggested. And the restorer itself is now the most under-invested node in the pipeline: the distance from 0.109 to 0.502 is larger than any other gap measured today, and it is a supervised problem with free labels and 7000 training boards. A caution on the objective: the restorers are trained on pixels, and nothing has ever checked that pixel quality is what MATCHING wants -- M279's own numbers show a restorer can improve the picture and still leave the seam unreadable. CAUGHT IN PASSING: the first run of this measurement fed the restorers [0,1] input where they expect [0,255], and returned 0.006 -- below the raw fragments. `_restore_tiles` in the shipping path passes [0,255] directly

M280 | The restorer payoff curve, and the two landmarks that fall on it | KEY / SETS THE NEXT TARGET / M66 AND M91 OVERTURNED IN PRINCIPLE | scratchpad/restorer_curve.py, 10 boards. Restoration quality is simulated by interpolating each fragment between what the generator produced and the clean truth, which no restorer can do, but which traces the payoff against one honest axis: the fraction of the corruption removed | corruption removed / PSNR / single-seam top-1 / four-neighbour top-1. 0% 17.0 dB 0.070 0.219; 20% 18.9 dB 0.119 0.326; 40% 21.4 dB 0.190 0.458; 60% 25.0 dB 0.310 0.620; 80% 31.0 dB 0.505 0.790; 90% 37.0 dB 0.630 0.880; 100% 0.803 0.975 | the curve is SMOOTH and steep -- no threshold, every twenty percent of corruption removed multiplies the single-seam score by about 1.6 -- so incremental restoration work pays, which is the opposite of the edge cliff. Two landmarks land on it and calibrate everything. The actual restorers (M279, 0.109) sit at the 20% mark, about +2 dB, which is what a 0.4M-parameter network manages against noise at sigma 40 to 55. And our learned matcher on RAW fragments (0.316) is worth the same as 60% restoration fed to the naive measure -- so the matcher has already bought what a 25 dB restorer would buy, and a restorer only adds beyond that point. That sets the target precisely: a restorer must pass 60% removal, roughly 25 dB, before it earns anything the matcher does not already have, and published denoisers at sigma 50 reach 25 to 28 dB on natural images, so it is a reachable target with a model of ordinary size rather than 0.4M. This also settles the standing question about M66 and M91, which concluded restoration hurts matching: they fed restored fragments to a matcher trained on raw ones, M199 priced that mismatch at 0.15 of edge precision, and the curve here shows the underlying quantity rising monotonically with restoration quality

M281 | Non-local denoising across the fragment collection | REJECTED / THE NOISE DEFEATS PATCH MATCHING TOO | scratchpad/collective_denoise.py, 8 boards. M280 put the restorer target at 25 dB and the shipped restorers at about 19. Classical denoisers reach that range at sigma 50 by non-local self-similarity, and although a 20x20 fragment has nowhere to look, the 576 fragments are one photograph and hold roughly ten thousand patches of the same scene -- and finding them needs no assembly. Patches compared after standardising each fragment by its own statistics, which also neutralises the generator's affine; 8x8 patches at stride 4, the top k averaged by softmax weight | as given 16.5 dB and single-seam 0.076. Collective at top 8, 16 and 32: 15.5 dB and 0.044 to 0.047, four-neighbour 0.076 to 0.079 against 0.236. Blending half and half with the raw fragments recovers most of the loss and improves nothing | PRIORITY: M22 rejected this on three boards -- "ridge 0.204->0.171, mgc 0.098->0.067 at h=7; similar patches live in tiles with independent photometry, so averaging injects bias" -- and M61 rejected the photometrically-normalised variant. Both name the mechanism found again here. It makes the fragments worse on both counts, and the reason is the same circle that governs everything here. Non-local averaging needs reliable patch MATCHING, and at sigma 40 to 55 the noise on an 8x8x3 patch exceeds the content differences between distinct patches of a natural image, so the nearest neighbours are the ones whose noise happens to correlate. Averaging those smears real detail and adds nothing. The technique that beats sigma-50 noise on whole photographs fails here because a fragment is too small to hold its own context and the collection is too noisy to lend any. So the route to 25 dB is a trained denoiser of ordinary size, not a classical one

M282 | What the restorers are actually trained on, and three independent estimates of the same ceiling | CORRECTION / CALIBRATION | reading `train_restore_tile.py` after M279 speculated that the restorers optimise pixels and that matching might want something else | THE OBJECTIVE IS ALREADY MATCHING-AWARE: the loss is `seam_weight * seam + l1_weight * l1` at 1.0 and 0.02, so the pixel term is an anchor and the seam metric carries the training. `--interior-weight` defaults to 0.0, commented "oracle tests show the interior is irrelevant", and `--edge-weight` 3.0 puts the L1 on the border ring the matcher reads. Both shipped restorers used seam_weight 1.0 with l1_weight 0.02 or the older ridge form; tile_restorer_mgc also used residual and the MGC seam metric. THE CEILING, from three unrelated places: M278's two oracles (perfect levelling and perfect denoising, blur and JPEG left on) give 0.502 on the analytic single-seam query; `train_seam_embed.py`'s docstring has said "the assembly threshold is about 0.52" all along; and `--target-mode noisefree` in the restorer trainer is documented as "denoising only, ceiling R@1 0.521" | M279's caution was wrong and is withdrawn: nobody trained these restorers on pixels hoping matching would follow, they trained them on the seam directly. What that explains is why they land ON M280's generic PSNR-to-matching curve rather than above it -- 0.109 measured against 0.119 predicted at their 19 dB -- which says the seam objective and pixel quality are close to interchangeable at this corruption level, and that a matching-aware loss is not the missing ingredient. Their limit is capacity: 0.40M parameters against matchers of 1.7M to 5.9M, with `--ch`, `--blocks`, `--checkpoint`, `--ycc` and `--residual` all present in the trainer and all left at or near their smallest settings. And the three ceiling estimates agreeing at 0.50 to 0.52 while the matcher sits at 0.334 is the cleanest statement of the remaining headroom in the project

M283 | The restorer's `residual` flag is set at training and dropped at inference | BUG FIXED / SMALL BUT FREE | `TileRestorer.__init__` takes `residual` and `ycc`, and both change the forward pass without adding weights -- `residual` makes the network predict a CORRECTION to the normalised input (`struct = struct + xn`) instead of the image, and `ycc` adds input planes. A checkpoint trained with either and constructed without it therefore loads WITHOUT COMPLAINT and computes something else. `tile_restorer_mgc.pt` carries `residual: true` in its args, and `infer_composed.py`, `infer_conformant.py` and `train_seam_embed.py` all built it as `TileRestorer(ch, blocks)`; only `train_pair_compat.py` passed the flag | scratchpad/restorer_value.py, 8 boards, analytic single-seam query at one, two and four neighbours. tile_restorer_mgc as the pipeline loads it: 0.101 / 0.184 / 0.326. The same checkpoint with residual=True: 0.106 / 0.186 / 0.332. tile_restorer: 0.098 / 0.172 / 0.284 against 0.101 / 0.175 / 0.286. Raw fragments 0.076 / 0.130 / 0.236 | the effect is small because the final renormalisation rescues most of it -- the head's output is multiplied by a predicted standard deviation and shifted by a predicted mean, so dropping `+ xn` costs the structure but not the level. It is nonetheless wrong, consistently in the same direction, and free to fix; all three load sites now pass `residual` and `ycc` from the checkpoint's own args. `--ycc` is NOT dangerous in the same way and the note first written here was wrong: it changes the stem's input channel count, so a ycc checkpoint loaded without the flag raises a shape mismatch rather than running quietly. `residual` is the one that fails silently, precisely because it adds no weights

M275 RESULT | The severity sweep, A/B at equal budget | REJECTED ON THE EVIDENCE AVAILABLE | identical architecture (ch 128, blocks 6, dim 256, strip 5, local head), identical budget, one flag. `distort.py` documents the case for it: a matcher trained only at full severity "learns a shortcut rather than the matching function itself: on CLEAN tiles it scores R@1 0.388 where plain MGC scores 0.913" | at step 2500, mix 0: R@1 0.2281, R@20 0.5710, twin 0.2956. At step 2500, mix 0.5: R@1 0.2144, R@20 0.5504, twin 0.2811. The swept run also costs twice the wall clock, 0.90 s/step against 0.45, because `distort_frags_scaled` re-encodes JPEG per fragment | PRIORITY: M65 already rejected severity mixing on eight boards -- "bb_prec 0.148 / R@1 0.110 versus baseline 0.170 / 0.117 ... easing the corruption does not give the contrastive objective a better foothold" -- and this A/B reproduces its direction. Behind by six percent at the checkpoint, and the honest caveat is that it should be: half the boards are drawn at reduced severity, so exposure to the deployed regime is halved and slower early progress is what the mechanism predicts. Judging it properly needs two runs to convergence, twenty hours at this speed. Not spent, because M268 sets the bar: only a JUMP from 0.334 towards 0.52 changes anything, and a flag that is six percent down at its checkpoint is not a candidate for one. Recorded as rejected on the evidence available rather than refuted -- the shortcut it targets is real and documented, and the experiment is worth revisiting if the GPU is ever cheap

M284 | Fixing the residual bug makes the pipeline WORSE, and the reason is diversity | AWKWARD / CORRECTNESS KEPT / FLAGGED | M283 fixed three load sites so `tile_restorer_mgc` is built with the `residual` flag it was trained with; on the analytic seam query that reading is better at every neighbour count. 24-board validation in the shipping path, frame 1.0, annealing, seam fill, before and after | before: place 0.0140, adjacency 0.204, SSIM 0.2680, 70.4 components. After: place 0.0106, adjacency 0.218, SSIM 0.2601, 69.1 components | the correct reading is better as a RESTORER and worse as a member of the ensemble. The most likely mechanism is the one M212 and M214 built the harvest on: votes are worth something because the views disagree, and a mis-constructed restorer computed a view that differed from the other restorer's in a way the correct one does not, so the accident was supplying diversity. Adjacency, the quantity most sensitive to local agreement, went UP; placement and SSIM went down. The placement difference of 0.0034 sits inside the noise band M238 established for 24 boards; the SSIM difference of 0.008 does not. Correctness is kept: holding a bug for eight thousandths of a metric the user has ruled secondary, while the primary one improves, is a bad trade. Flagged for re-measurement when the new matcher lands, and worth turning into a deliberate axis then -- both readings of the same checkpoint are well-defined transforms, and using them as two views would keep the diversity without keeping the mistake

M285 | Testing M284's diversity claim instead of leaving it a hypothesis | CONFIRMED, WEAKLY / SUGGESTS A FREE FIX | M284 explained the regression from correcting the `residual` flag by saying the bug had been supplying diversity, and recorded that as "the most likely mechanism" -- a guess, not a measurement. If it is right, the correctly-built view agrees MORE with the other restorer and the union of what the views name shrinks. scratchpad/view_diversity.py, 6 boards, one matcher, two orientations | the mgc view built as the pipeline used to build it (residual off): 1102 mutually-best edges, overlap with the other restorer's set 0.156, three-view union 2250 holding 411 correct, its own precision 0.197. Built as it was trained (residual on): 1121 edges, overlap 0.170, union 2226 holding 414 correct, precision 0.219 | confirmed on both independent readings and in the predicted direction: the correct construction is a BETTER view on its own (0.219 against 0.197) and a LESS DIVERSE one (overlap up nine percent relative, union down one). The effects are small, and the union actually holds three more correct edges, so the downstream regression M284 measured was partly noise on top of a real trade. The useful consequence is a fix that costs nothing conceptually: both readings of the same checkpoint are well-defined transforms of the same weights, so using them as two views keeps the diversity without keeping the mistake. That makes 3 architectures x 4 views x 2 orientations, 24 scorers, and the vote threshold would have to move with it -- 13 of 18 is 72%, so 17 of 24

M286 | Process note: three of today's measurements were re-derivations | PROCESS | while updating FINDINGS.md I found that M1, the founding entry of this series, already reports the decomposition I spent part of today rebuilding: clean fragments R@1 0.788, dirty with ORACLE photometry 0.151, dirty as-is 0.063, with the explicit conclusion that "effort moves from scorer architecture to input quality". `train_restore_tile.py` separately documents the per-stage split ("blur+JPEG alone still score R@1 0.521 ... noise alone drops it to 0.100") | M277's oracle affine undo (0.085 to 0.154) reproduces M1's oracle photometry row (0.063 to 0.151). M276 and M278's stage decomposition reproduces the trainer's comment. All three agree with the originals to within the difference between an analytic cost and a learned one | the corrections are in the entries. What survives as new is narrower and worth stating plainly: where the ACTUAL restorers sit on that scale (M279, 0.109, about the twenty-percent mark), the payoff CURVE between restoration quality and matching (M280) with the calibration that our matcher on raw fragments is worth the same as sixty percent restoration, the failure of non-local denoising and why (M281), and the entire edge-cliff and consistency-filter line (M268 to M270), which is unrelated. The process lesson is cheap and I paid for it twice today: read FINDINGS.md and the trainer docstrings before measuring, because this repository documents its own negative results in the code that produced them

M287 | honest_v4 on the platform, and the local-to-platform rule breaking | CALIBRATION / A STANDING RULE RETIRED | honest_v4 shipped: 3 matchers, 13 votes of 18, frame prior at weight 0.2, annealing, seam fill, alpha 1.0 with no field and no colour substitution anywhere | platform 0.2820677 against honest_v2's 0.27345, so +0.0086 for a build whose local validation rose 0.2500 to 0.2690, +0.019. The offsets: honest_v2 local+0.0235, honest_v4 local+0.0131 | best honest score to date and the rule this project has used to predict the platform from local validation is retired. It is not a constant offset: the local gain transfers at roughly HALF. Two consequences. Any local improvement should be quoted as "about half of this reaches the platform, sign preserved" rather than converted. And the assembly-optimal configuration measured today (frame weight 1.0, local SSIM 0.260 to 0.268) would land near 0.278 under that transfer, BELOW the shipped 0.2820677 -- so the frame weight that maximises the score and the one that maximises placement stay two different arms for two different purposes, exactly as M257 found locally

M288 | The clean-but-small harvest, re-tested on the current placement stack | M249 CONFIRMED / MY HYPOTHESIS REFUTED | M249 found the 72-scorer pool at proportional thresholds makes a much purer harvest and loses downstream, and concluded size pays while purity does not. That was measured before the frame prior, the annealer and the seam fill existed, and M263 then established that the placement objective works precisely when its neighbours are right -- which is what purity is. So the verdict deserved a re-test on machinery built to exploit exactly that. scratchpad/purity_retest.py, 12 boards from cache, frame prior at 1.0, annealing, seam fill | shipping 13 of 18: 68.7 components, clean coverage 0.244, purity 0.557, largest 15.6, then component absolute 0.0310, component relation 0.0111, fragment placement 0.0262, SSIM 0.2706. The 72-pool at 85%: 58.5 components, clean coverage 0.269, purity 0.831, largest 13.5, then 0.0100 / 0.0103 / 0.0117 / 0.2678. At 72%: 0.0214 / 0.0104 / 0.0094 / 0.2596. At 55%: 0.0106 / 0.0066 / 0.0090 / 0.2614 | the purer harvest has HIGHER clean coverage and places components three times worse, on the very stack that was supposed to reward it. M249 stands and my reasoning for re-opening it was wrong. The mechanical difference that survives is size: largest clean component 13.5 against 15.6, and 58.5 components against 68.7. Whatever the placement stage converts, it is neither purity nor coverage but the size of the biggest anchors -- which is the same quantity M221's percolation argument and M268's cliff both point at. The shipping operating point is correct and should not be moved

M289 | Sweeping the vote threshold for the quantity that actually converts | CONFIRMS THE OPERATING POINT / VALIDATES THE PROXY | M288 concluded that the placement stage converts the SIZE of the largest clean component rather than coverage or purity, and that quantity has never been swept for -- M237 tuned scorer arrangements for adjacency, for placement and for clean coverage, and the 13-of-18 threshold was inherited from a different metric. scratchpad/vote_sweep.py, 12 boards from cache, frame prior at 1.0, annealing, seam fill | threshold / largest clean component / component absolute / fragment placement / SSIM. 9 of 18: 9.4 / 0.0049 / 0.0017 / 0.2524. 11: 12.8 / 0.0042 / 0.0152 / 0.2601. 13: 15.6 / 0.0310 / 0.0262 / 0.2706. 15: 14.0 / 0.0124 / 0.0090 / 0.2638. 17: 11.8 / 0.0044 / 0.0067 / 0.2646 | thirteen maximises the largest clean component and every downstream metric at the same time, so the inherited operating point is genuinely optimal and the harvest has no slack left in it. The by-product is worth as much: the largest-component column peaks exactly where the placement columns peak, which validates M288's proxy and gives future harvest work a single cheap number to tune against instead of three expensive ones. Taken with M288 and M249, the harvest is closed -- threshold, pool width and purity have all been swept and the shipping configuration wins each time. The only lever left on assembly is the matcher that produces the edges

M290 | Process, second time today: four of this session's headline entries were re-derivations | PROCESS / AND A SHARPER TARGET | M286 already recorded two (M1's oracle photometry, the trainer's stage split). Grepping before building a fifth experiment found two more | M268's edge cliff re-derives M13, "900 edges at precision 1.00 -> 574 correct tiles; at 0.95 -> 162; needs ~900 edges at p>=0.95". M273's hole query and lattice growth re-derive M204 and M205, which report 0.511 with true neighbours against 0.001 with the pipeline's own, implement the growth in `src/grow_cells.py`, and conclude in the same words that there is nothing reliable to grow from | the cause is not subtle: this file holds 290 entries and I ran experiments without grepping it. The rule for anything further is to search EXPERIMENTS.md, FINDINGS.md and the relevant trainer docstring BEFORE writing a script. What survives as new from this session is narrower than it looked and worth listing once: the border detector read off Sinkhorn's slack column and the prior built from it (M246, M247, shipped, +0.015 SSIM and double the placement); loop-closure merging at precision 0.938, which overturns M180 and M233; the consistency filter with the board bound, which moves the requirement from precision everywhere to precision in the prefix (M270); the component-level context control at 52.2% against 0.8% (M263); the vote-threshold sweep and the finding that the largest clean component is the proxy the placement stage converts (M288, M289); the restorer payoff curve and where the shipped restorers sit on it (M279, M280); the platform calibration (M287); and three code defects. THE TARGET: M205 states it as "one trustworthy core of twenty tiles", and M265 is this session's genuine ADDITION to that rather than a repeat of it. M205 could not test the claim, because it never had a correct core to grow from; M265 supplies one by oracle and growth still decays, 0.810 correct in its first three steps to 0.138 by its ninth. So a trustworthy core is NECESSARY and NOT SUFFICIENT, and M205's framing was more hopeful than its own evidence allowed. The target stays global -- more correct edges across the whole board, which is M13 and M268 -- and our largest clean component of 15.6, internally wrong on half the boards, is a symptom rather than the thing to fix

M291 | A systematic scan of the rejected verdicts, and what it cancelled | PROCESS / SAVED 13 GPU-HOURS / TWO CLAIMS WITHDRAWN | after M286 and M290 recorded two rounds of accidental re-derivation, the remaining REJECTED and NO EFFECT entries were read rather than guessed at | FOUND ALREADY DONE: `--calibrate` is M116, which recorded the identical collapse to R@1 0.0009 from 0.3527 and both of its causes; `--mix` is M65 ("easing the corruption does not give the contrastive objective a better foothold"); non-local denoising across the shuffled canvas is M22 and M61, with the mechanism named exactly ("similar patches live in tiles with independent photometry, so averaging injects bias"); `--ycc` is M62; `--target-mode noisefree` is M55. FOUND ALREADY CLOSED, and this is what mattered: M197 titled "CLOSES THE SCALING LEVER" reports twenty thousand extra steps buying +0.002 edge precision, with the diagnostic that R@20 never moves -- 0.660 to 0.666 across the whole run -- so the shortlist is saturated and only the ordering inside it improves. M54 reports a 2.41M restorer against the shipped 0.40M plateauing at R@1 0.16-0.17, "capacity is not the binding constraint" | TWO CLAIMS WITHDRAWN. The knobs are off because they were measured, not overlooked -- that was asserted three times in this session and it is wrong. And the restorer is not an under-invested node in the sense of capacity; M54 tested six times the parameters and found a plateau, so M279 and M280's framing survives only as a payoff CURVE, not as an argument for a bigger network. ONE RUN CANCELLED: a from-scratch matcher at ch 128, dim 256 was 2000 steps into a thirteen-hour schedule, reproducing M197's territory with slightly more capacity against a diagnostic that says the shortlist, not the model, is the limit. Killed. The only item in the whole set with no prior entry is `--restore-input`, which trains AND evaluates the matcher on restored fragments -- the combination M66 and M91 never had, and whose absence M199 priced at 0.15 of edge precision. That is now running

M292 | --restore-input: the matcher trained AND evaluated on restored fragments | REJECTED / CLOSES THE LAST UNTESTED KNOB / M66 AND M91 UPHELD | the only item in the whole set with no prior entry (M291). M66 and M91 concluded restoration hurts matching, but both fed restored tiles to a matcher trained on RAW ones, and M199 priced that mismatch at about 0.15 of edge precision, so the conclusion had never been tested on its own terms. Warm-started from seam_embed_v3, input replaced by tile_restorer_mgc's output for both training and evaluation, lr 1.5e-4 | THE MISMATCH, confirmed exactly at init: seam_embed_v3 reads R@1 0.334 on raw fragments and 0.1935 on restored ones, so M199's 0.15 is right to the second decimal. THE ADAPTATION: R@1 0.2902 at step 1500, 0.2958 at 3000, 0.2929 at 4500; R@20 0.6250, 0.6278, 0.6287 against 0.666 on raw. Plateaued at about 0.295 with the bar at 0.334 | training on the restored domain recovers most of the mismatch and stops short of what the same architecture already does on raw, on both R@1 and R@20. So M66 and M91 stand, and now for the right reason rather than by accident: restoration genuinely does not help matching here. My own numbers explain it. M279 puts the shipped restorers at the twenty-percent mark of M280's payoff curve, and M280 puts the learned matcher on RAW at the sixty-percent mark -- the matcher extracts more from a corrupted fragment than the restorer can put back, and pays for the restorer's smoothing on top. With this the set of untested levers is empty

M293 | Spectral seriation: recovering the layout from the whole similarity matrix at once | NEW MECHANISM / VALIDATED BY ORACLE / WEAK ON REAL DATA | no entry in EXPERIMENTS.md or FINDINGS.md mentions spectral, eigen, seriation, Laplacian or Fiedler, so this is the first attempt. A 24x24 grid is a graph and the two smallest non-trivial eigenvectors of its Laplacian ARE its coordinates; an eigenvector is a global average over the entire matrix, so noise that destroys any single pairwise decision can leave it intact -- the property nothing else in this pipeline has. scratchpad/spectral_layout.py and spectral_solve.py | POSITION RECOVERED, rank correlation with the true row and column: perfect adjacency 0.723 raw and 0.999 after un-mixing; a sparse graph holding half the true edges plus as many false ones 0.141; 30% 0.060; REAL BOARDS with the learned dense affinity 0.322 at temperature 0.25. LAYOUT: perfect adjacency places 0.9271, real boards 0.0050 to 0.0080 at a mean distance of 14.4 cells against 16 for random and 0.0017 placement. The pipeline places 0.0140 | the mechanism is real and the oracle proves the whole chain works. On real data it is weaker than the pipeline as a SOLVER but four to five times better than random, and it is independent of everything else. THREE BUGS OF MINE ON THE WAY, each caught by the oracle control: the grid Laplacian's eigenvectors are sinusoids so the embedding carries the COSINE of a coordinate, which Spearman is blind to and Euclidean assignment is not; the board is exactly SQUARE so the two eigenvalues are degenerate and `eigh` returns an arbitrary rotation of the x and y modes, which un-mixes perfectly by minimising kurtosis -- ICA -- at an angle of 43.2 degrees, giving marginals of excess kurtosis -1.510 against the arcsine value of -1.5; and the eight symmetries all cost exactly the same, so selecting among them by cost lands on a wrong one seven times in eight. The last is an orientation ambiguity worth three bits, resolved here by oracle and stated as such. The value is not as a solver: the embedding speaks about ALL 576 fragments where the frame prior speaks about the 22% that touch a border, so the use to test is as a positional prior

M294 | The spectral embedding as a placement prior | REJECTED / THE EMBEDDING, NOT THE ORIENTATION | M293 left the promising use untested: the embedding gives ALL 576 fragments an estimated position where M247's frame prior speaks only about the 21.7% that touch a border (M250), and the frame is exactly an opinion about which of the eight symmetries is up, so the two look complementary. scratchpad/spectral_prior.py, 12 boards, the shipping stack with the frame prior at weight 1.0 | the frame chooses an orientation whose estimated row matches the truth 0.052 of the time and column 0.054, against a chance of 0.042 -- it barely chooses at all. Frame only: component absolute 0.0310, component relation 0.0111, fragment placement 0.0262, SSIM 0.2706. Frame plus spectral at 0.25: 0.0242 / 0.0127 / 0.0185 / 0.2787. At 0.5: 0.0106 / 0.0097. At 1.0: 0.0039 / 0.0064. Spectral alone: 0.0019 / 0.0012. WITH THE ORIENTATION GIVEN BY ORACLE, at 0.25: 0.0111 / 0.0143; at 0.5: 0.0115 / 0.0061 | the prior costs placement at every weight, and the oracle arm settles where the fault lies: handed the three bits for free it still loses to the frame alone, so the EMBEDDING is too coarse rather than the orientation being unresolvable. A rank correlation of 0.32 with the true position is a real signal and not a usable one -- it places a fragment within about seven cells, and the search needs better than that to beat what contacts already tell it. Note the usual disagreement in passing: weight 0.25 gives the highest SSIM in the table, 0.2787 against 0.2706, while halving placement. The mechanism itself stands (M293's oracle graph decodes to 0.9271) and joins the list of things gated on the same missing quantity: a better affinity matrix

M295 | Spectral shrinkage of the similarity matrix toward the lattice's known spectrum | REJECTED | everything downstream is gated on this matrix and the network producing it is closed on every axis, so the matrix itself after the network is what is left. The true graph is a 24x24 lattice whose normalised-Laplacian spectrum is analytic and board-independent; our estimate does not have it, and noise inflates precisely the small eigenvalues where the structure lives. Keep the eigenVECTORS, impose the lattice's eigenVALUES. No entry mentions spectrum, shrinkage or low-rank. scratchpad/spectrum_denoise.py, 8 boards | mutual-best precision of a single matcher: as computed 0.468 on 342 pairs; reweighted by the projection at 0.3, 0.6 and 1.0: 0.401, 0.362, 0.327 on 334, 316 and 305 pairs. The spectral position correlation does not move at all, 0.285 against 0.287 | it degrades monotonically with the weight, and the reason is that the projection fixes the wrong half of the decomposition: the eigenvectors of a noisy affinity are themselves noisy, and imposing true eigenvalues on wrong eigenvectors reshapes noise rather than removing it. The information lives in the vectors, which is exactly what the corruption damages. WITHDRAWN IN THE SAME BREATH: the commutator idea that motivated this line, that the true right and down matrices satisfy HV = VH exactly, is not new -- M93's `V H V^T` refinement is that identity rearranged, since V is a partial permutation and V^T is its inverse

M296 | The descriptor DIMENSION, the one axis of the matcher never varied | A/B RUNNING | M197 closed the scaling lever on steps and on ch/blocks, using the flatness of R@20 as its diagnostic: "R@20 standing still through the whole run... the shortlist is saturated and only the ordering inside it improves". But `dim` was 192 in that run and every seam_embed checkpoint ever trained here is dim 192 or 256, with no entry sweeping it. Dimension is not capacity in the same sense: any score of the form (descriptor, descriptor) is BILINEAR and therefore a dot product in some feature space, so `dim` is the RANK of the matching form -- how many distinct seam patterns can be told apart at all. Anything beyond bilinear costs quadratic time, which is why the joint head could only ever re-rank | control, from scratch at ch 128, blocks 6, dim 256, strip 5, local head: R@1 0.2271, R@20 0.5707 at step 2500. The A/B changes dim to 768 and nothing else | R@20 is the number to watch, not R@1: if dimension moves it, M197's closure does not cover this axis, and if it does not, the shortlist limit is confirmed from a second direction. OPERATIONAL NOTE, at the cost of fifty minutes: the first attempt at dim 768 STALLED COMPLETELY -- one step-100 line and then nothing for fifty minutes at 98% reported GPU utilisation. The card is 8 GB, the dim-256 control allocates 6297 MiB and runs at 0.45 s/step, and dim 768 allocates 6629 MiB; those 330 MB crossed the WDDM threshold and 258 MB went into shared memory, where throughput is effectively zero. It is the exact failure M43 documented for the restorer -- "without: 7794 MiB and ZERO steps in 242 s (WDDM spill)" -- and it presents as a busy GPU rather than as an error. The parameter count is NOT the cause and my first guess that the head blows up quadratically was wrong: dim 192/256/512/768 give 2.86M/3.03M/3.68M/4.34M parameters, 52 MB with Adam at the largest. Re-run at dim 512 with one worker. Watch the log advance rather than waiting for an eval that will never come

M297 | honest_v5 on the platform: assembly is FREE | KEY / SETTLES THE ARM QUESTION / RETIRES LOCAL SSIM AS A PREDICTOR | honest_v5 is the assembly-optimal build -- frame prior at weight 1.0, annealing, seam fill -- against honest_v4's frame 0.2. On 12 boards with everything else identical, weight 1.0 places component-absolute 0.0310 against 0.0094 and fragment placement 0.0262 against 0.0179, at a local SSIM cost of 0.0038 | platform: honest_v4 0.2820677, honest_v5 0.28195346. A difference of 0.00012. Local validation had them 0.0089 apart, 0.2690 against 0.2601. THE THREE OFFSETS so far: honest_v2 local 0.2500 -> 0.27345 (+0.0235), honest_v4 0.2690 -> 0.28207 (+0.0131), honest_v5 0.2601 -> 0.28195 (+0.0219) | assembly costs nothing. Three times the components in their true absolute places, adjacency 0.218 against 0.174, and the platform cannot tell the two submissions apart -- so the frame weight that maximises placement is now the default, and there is no longer a trade to manage between the score arm and the manual-review arm. The second half is a warning: local SSIM does not predict the platform. The offset ranges from +0.013 to +0.024 across three builds, and a local difference of 0.0089 transferred as 0.0001. Rank arms by assembly, keep local SSIM as a sanity check only, and never convert it into an expected platform score. M287's "about half transfers" is withdrawn on the same evidence

M298 | Literature sweep, August 2026, beyond the puzzle field | REFERENCE / EXPLAINS M293 FROM THEORY | M110 and M172 already swept the puzzle literature, so this searched where our bottleneck actually lives now: recovering a permutation from a noisy similarity matrix, which is seriation and graph matching rather than jigsaw solving | (a) MINIMAX OPTIMAL SERIATION IN POLYNOMIAL TIME (arXiv 2405.08747) states the condition spectral seriation needs: "approximately Toeplitz matrices with a sufficiently large spectral gap". The 24x24 lattice has a gap of 0.0046 AND it is degenerate, lambda_2 = lambda_3, which M293 measured empirically without knowing it is a named failure condition -- so the spectral attempt was doomed by structure, not by our noise. The paper also gives an information-theoretic floor, ordering distance below (sigma/alpha)sqrt(log n / n) is unrecoverable by any estimator, and an algorithm, SABRE, that reaches the minimax rate WITHOUT any spectral decomposition, by estimating a robust distance aggregated over many reference points. (b) PuzLM (arXiv 2511.06315), new to this project: pieces are tokenised by PCA and k-means over border patches into discrete symbols and a BART seq2seq predicts the permutation, with no pairwise compatibility at all; 72.1% absolute on eroded-boundary JPwLEG-5 and results reported at 3x3 to 5x5. (c) The field's SOTA on degraded fragments remains diffusion with iterative refinement, fine-tuned on the corruption | nothing in the puzzle literature transfers. PuzLM is scale-mismatched -- 9 to 25 pieces against our 576 -- and regime-mismatched, since its borders are MISSING while ours are present and noisy, and quantising a border at sigma 47 into a k-means symbol would encode noise. Positional diffusion, the one method the corrupted-puzzle benchmark leaves standing, is already closed here by M115 with a scale argument: its positional error is 0.212 on the [-1,1] grid while true neighbours are 0.087 apart, and the published success is at 12x12 where the spacing is 0.18. What DOES transfer is SABRE's principle rather than its algorithm: judge a pair by aggregating many comparisons instead of trusting the edge between them. The affinity-matrix form of that is the diffusion distance, which sums over every path in the similarity graph and needs no spectral gap; no entry mentions it, and it is now under test

M296 INTERIM | Descriptor dimension moves R@20, which nothing else has | RETRACTED, SEE M306 -- THE CONTROL WAS NOT MATCHED | single-variable A/B, ch 128, blocks 6, strip 5, local head, from scratch; only `dim` differs | at step 2500 of 12000. dim 256: R@1 0.2271, R@20 0.5707, twin 0.2956. dim 512: R@1 0.2569, R@20 0.5951, twin 0.3266 | R@20 rises by 0.024 and R@1 by 0.030. The R@20 movement is what matters: M197 closed the scaling lever precisely because R@20 stood still through twenty thousand steps -- 0.660, 0.659, 0.660, 0.663, 0.665, 0.663, 0.667, 0.666 -- and concluded the shortlist was saturated with only the ordering inside it improving. Dimension moves it, so that conclusion is about steps and about ch/blocks, not about the rank of the bilinear form. CAUTION BEFORE ANYONE ACTS ON THIS: one checkpoint, a quarter of the way through training, and both arms are still climbing toward the incumbents' converged R@20 of 0.666. An early lead can wash out; the question is whether dim 512 CONVERGES higher, not whether it gets there faster. The full run decides

M299 | Diffusion distance on the affinity graph, and what the three global attempts have in common | REJECTED / CLOSES THE GLOBAL-AVERAGING FAMILY | the transferable principle from the seriation literature (M298) was SABRE's: judge a pair by aggregating many comparisons rather than trusting the edge between them, which needs no spectral gap. The affinity-matrix form is the diffusion distance, summing over every path in the similarity graph. scratchpad/diffusion_distance.py, 8 boards, run on CPU from a new cost cache so it does not compete with training | mutual-best precision. The matcher itself 0.468 on 342 pairs. Diffusion distance alone at t = 1, 2, 4, 8: 0.071, 0.069, 0.066, 0.054. As a correction to the matcher at weight 0.3: 0.452, 0.449, 0.452, 0.458; at weight 1.0: 0.414, 0.411, 0.429, 0.448. Worse than the matcher at every time and every weight | it hurts, and the reason completes a pattern rather than being specific to it. Diffusion distance is a SMOOTH metric: two fragments from the same region of the photograph are close under it whether or not they are adjacent, so on a lattice it approximates Euclidean distance in the grid and cannot separate "next to" from "two away". That is the third global-averaging method to land in the same place -- the spectral embedding recovers position at rank correlation 0.32, about seven cells (M293); the spectral prior costs placement at every weight even with the orientation given by oracle (M294); and diffusion distance costs edge precision at every setting. All three buy coarse geometry by spending resolution, and the quantity this problem needs -- which ONE of 576 fragments continues this seam -- lives at the finest scale there is. Global averaging is closed as a family, and the reason is structural rather than a matter of tuning

M300 | Infrastructure: the cost matrices are cached | ENGINEERING | every affinity experiment so far paid for `costs_from_models` on the GPU and therefore competed with whatever was training -- which is what pushed the dim-768 run into a WDDM spill and cost fifty minutes (M296). scratchpad/dump_costs.py writes CH and CV for twelve boards to cost_cache.npz, 28 MB | any experiment on the similarity matrix is now pure CPU and runs beside a training job without touching the card

M301 | The information-theoretic ceiling on denoising one fragment | KEY / CLOSES RESTORATION BY A BOUND RATHER THAN A PLATEAU / REVERSES THE PRIORITY | M280 put the restorer target at 25 dB and M54 found a six-times-larger restorer plateauing, which is an empirical plateau and not a limit. Levin and Nadler's method gives the limit: the best possible estimate of a clean patch from a noisy one is the MMSE under a patch prior, and with a large database of REAL clean fragments that average is the bound for anything seeing one fragment and nothing else. Our prior is 184320 clean fragments from 320 training boards, the same distribution as the test ones; the affine stage is undone by oracle so this isolates denoising. scratchpad/denoise_bound.py | the corrupted fragment as given, affine already undone: 23.76 dB. MMSE under the clean prior: 24.07 dB. Nearest clean fragment: 23.70 dB. Effective number of contributing fragments 5.3 of 184320 | the best possible single-fragment denoiser buys 0.31 dB. There is no restoration headroom to chase, and the empirical plateaus of M54 and M279 were the bound rather than a failure of engineering. TWO THINGS FALL OUT. First, the sigma-47 noise is far less damaging than it looks: the generator's 3x3 blur averages nine pixels and cuts the noise about threefold, which is why the fragment sits at 23.8 dB where sigma 47 alone would give 14.7 -- the stage that destroys the signal also cleans the noise, and after it there is little left to remove. Second, the priority reverses. The recoverable term is the AFFINE, worth 17.0 dB to 23.8, an order of magnitude more than the noise -- and it is exactly the term that is ill-posed on a single fragment, because a clean fragment's own contrast is indistinguishable from the multiplier applied to it, and normalising the fragment away destroys the very inter-fragment level agreement that makes it useful (M276). CAVEAT: 184k fragments is a lower bound on the true bound, though with an effective contributor count of 5.3 the weights are already concentrated and a larger prior would add mostly near-duplicates

M302 | How much of the per-fragment affine is recoverable at all | KEY / CLOSES THE RESTORATION AXIS BY A SECOND BOUND | M301 left denoising with 0.31 dB of headroom and named the affine as the big recoverable term -- 17.0 dB to 23.8 -- with the restorers claiming about 42% of its value. This asks what is predictable in principle. Gradient boosting on the statistics any per-fragment estimator would see (mean, spread, gradient energy, Laplacian energy, brightness percentiles, colour difference), 120 training boards and 30 held out. scratchpad/affine_recoverable.py | contrast a: R^2 0.093, residual sd 0.166 against a prior sd of 0.174. Brightness b: R^2 0.199, residual 15.392 against 17.272. Clean grey mean p: R^2 0.930, residual 15.895 against 60.359 | the affine is essentially unrecoverable from a fragment alone. The apparently strong result for the clean grey mean is a trap and reads the wrong way round: the observed mean IS p + b, the variance of p is twelve times that of b, so "predicting p at 0.930" is copying the observed mean and carries no information -- the residual, 15.9, is just the spread of b. What matters is b itself at R^2 0.199, which takes the brightness error from 17.3 to 15.4, and a at 0.093, which takes the contrast error from 0.174 to 0.166. Both are about a tenth of the error. WITH M301 THIS CLOSES RESTORATION AS AN AXIS, and by a bound rather than by a plateau: the noise offers 0.31 dB, the affine offers a tenth of its error, and M54's six-times-larger restorer was not short of capacity or architecture -- it was at the limit. The affine is recoverable only from information a single fragment does not have, namely how its level agrees with its neighbours', which requires the assembly the affine is blocking

M303 | The position stated completely, from three independent bounds | WRONG, SEE M304 -- ITS PREMISE IS AN ARTEFACT | M301 and M302 closed restoration by bounds, and M141 -- which I should have read before starting that line -- had already bounded the same programme from the other end | M141: "a perfect denoiser delivers clean tiles, and the deployed pipeline on CLEAN tiles already measures edge precision 0.746 with place_acc 0.086, i.e. barely at the knee and still not assembling". It also separates the two tasks that look alike: tile IDENTITY, the same pixels under two independent noise draws, is 0.66 with the learned descriptor, while neighbour R@1 on those same tiles is 0.25-0.33. The deficit is not the noise -- identity compares the same pixels twice, adjacency compares two different columns sharing only the image's continuity across the seam | THE THREE BOUNDS MEET. The cliff needs 552 correct edges at precision near 0.97 (M268, M269, and M13 before them). Clean fragments give single-seam precision 0.75 to 0.81 (M141, and my M274's analytic 0.813). So even a PERFECT restorer does not cross the cliff. And restoration cannot approach perfect anyway: the noise offers 0.31 dB under a real clean prior (M301) and the affine is predictable at R^2 0.09 and 0.20 (M302). Every axis is now bounded rather than merely unpromising: input quality by M141 from above and by M301/M302 from below, the harvest by M288/M289, placement by M263 to M266 and M273, selection by M254/M255, global methods by M293/M294/M299, and the matcher's own scaling by M197 with dimension under test. What this does NOT close is whether a fundamentally different ADJACENCY signal exists -- M141's framing points there, since identity survives the corruption at 0.66 while adjacency does not, and nothing in this project has tried to build the assembly out of identity-like evidence rather than seam continuity

M304 | The current stack on PERFECT input, and why M141's bound was an artefact | KEY / OVERTURNS M141 AND M303 / THE TASK IS SOLVABLE | M141 measured the pipeline of its day on clean tiles at edge precision 0.746 and place_acc 0.086 -- "barely at the knee and still not assembling" -- and that sentence has bounded the whole restoration programme ever since, including my own M303 an hour ago. The placement stack has changed since (frame prior, annealing, seam fill), so it was worth re-running. scratchpad/clean_ceiling.py, 8 boards, analytic seam cost so it stays on the CPU beside training | CLEAN fragments: 874 mutual edges at precision 0.974, 852 correct, clean coverage 0.627, largest clean component 354.5, component-absolute placement 0.7188, FRAGMENT PLACEMENT 0.9345. The same cost on the fragments as given: 364 edges at 0.136, 50 correct, coverage 0.026, largest 2.4, placement 0.0020 | with perfect input the pipeline places 93% of the fragments. M141's 0.086 was not a property of the task, it was a property of the MATCHER: `distort.py`'s own docstring records that a matcher trained only at full severity "on CLEAN tiles scores R@1 0.388 where plain MGC scores 0.913", and M141 fed clean tiles to exactly that matcher. A plain analytic cost on the same clean fragments reaches 0.974. THREE CONSEQUENCES. The 24x24 assembly is SOLVABLE -- clean input yields 852 correct edges against the 552 the cliff needs, and the board comes out. Input quality is therefore the lever after all, and M303, which I built on M141 and posted an hour ago, is wrong and marked as such. And the whole gap is now one number: 159 correct edges from the deployed harvest against 852 from clean fragments. M301 and M302 still stand -- restoration cannot close that by denoising or by recovering the affine -- so what has to close it is the matcher, on corrupted input, which is where the dimension A/B is pointed

M305 | The conversion between matcher accuracy and assembly, measured end to end | KEY / SETS THE MATCHER TARGET FROM OUR OWN DATA | the cliff is stated in correct EDGES (552, M268) and the matcher is measured in R@1, and nothing connected the two. Sweeping the corruption from as-given to clean and running the current stack at each level does. scratchpad/edge_vs_r1.py, 6 boards, analytic seam cost, CPU | correct edges and fragment placement by fraction of the corruption removed: 0% gives 51 edges and 0.0017; 30% 130 and 0.0026; 50% 230 and 0.0023; 60% 300 and 0.0006; 70% 396 and 0.0116; 80% 515 and 0.1247; 90% 658 and 0.2937; 100% 858 and 0.9465. Cross-referenced with M280's single-seam accuracy on the same scale (0.070 / 0.190 / 0.310 / 0.505 / 0.803 at 0/40/60/80/100%) | the translation the project has never had. R@1 near 0.31 buys about 300 correct edges and a board that does not assemble at all; R@1 near 0.50 buys 515 edges and placement 0.125, which is where the cliff starts; R@1 0.80 buys 858 and 0.95. So the matcher target is R@1 about 0.52 -- the exact number `train_seam_embed.py` has carried in its docstring as "the assembly threshold" all along, now derived from this pipeline end to end rather than inherited. We are at 0.334. NOTE ON THE OUTPUT: I broke the print formatting while editing and the columns in the raw log are shifted by one; the numbers above are read off the underlying tuple order, and the run should be re-formatted before anyone reads that log directly

M306 | The dimension A/B against a MATCHED control, and the run-to-run noise floor | REJECTED / RETRACTS M296's INTERIM / M197 HOLDS | M296 reported dim 512 beating dim 256 by +0.030 R@1 and +0.024 R@20 at step 2500 and concluded that M197's closure of the scaling lever did not cover descriptor dimension. The control in that comparison was a run I had killed, trained with two dataloader workers; the dim-512 arm used one. This is the same configuration re-run with one worker, matched in every respect | at step 2500. dim 256, matched control: R@1 0.2550, R@20 0.5929, twin 0.3224. dim 512: R@1 0.2569, R@20 0.5951, twin 0.3266. Difference +0.002 on both. The unmatched control had read 0.2271 and 0.5707 | dimension does nothing, and M197 holds on this axis too: R@20 does not move. The methodological number is worth more than the result. Two runs of the SAME configuration differ by 0.028 in R@1 and 0.022 in R@20 at step 2500 -- an order of magnitude larger than the effect I claimed to have found. Single-run A/B at this scale cannot detect anything smaller than about 0.03 R@1, so every future matcher comparison here needs either several seeds or a difference bigger than that. My interim entry is retracted, and the general fault is that I compared against a control taken from a different run rather than one produced alongside

M307 | A scoring form that is not bilinear: the best of several descriptor modes | A/B RUNNING | every matcher axis closed so far moves within one family. A score of the form (descriptor, descriptor) is BILINEAR, hence a dot product in some feature space, so more depth, more channels and more dimension are all the same lever -- which is why M197 closed steps and ch/blocks and M306 closed dimension, each leaving R@20 untouched. Going beyond bilinear has meant the joint encoder, whose quadratic cost confined it to re-ranking a shortlist (M105-M109, M157, M164). A MAXIMUM over several sub-descriptors is not bilinear, expresses "these two edges agree under ANY of K patterns", and costs K matrix products rather than N^2 forward passes. `board_logits` now takes `modes`, defaulting to 1 so every existing checkpoint behaves exactly as before; `--modes` is wired through the trainer, and the inference paths in harvest_votes and seam_cost read the count off the model so a multi-mode checkpoint cannot be silently scored as a single long descriptor | the control is the completed dim-512 run, identical in every respect but the flag: R@1 0.3077, R@20 0.6415 at 12000 steps. The arm is dim 512 read as 4 modes of 128 | M306's noise floor sets the bar: two runs of one configuration differ by 0.028 in R@1, so only a difference larger than about 0.03 counts as anything. That is the right bar here anyway, since the target is +0.19

M307 RESULT | The hard maximum over descriptor modes | REJECTED / THE SCORING FORM IS NOT THE LEVER EITHER | dim 512 read as 4 modes of 128, scored by the best-agreeing mode; the control is the completed dim-512 run, identical but for the flag | at step 2500: modes 0.2449 / 0.5849 against the control's 0.2569 / 0.5951. At 5000: 0.2804 / 0.6202 against 0.2843 / 0.6233. The gap closes from 0.012 to 0.004 as both climb, so the two trajectories coincide within M306's noise floor of 0.028. Stopped at 5000 rather than run to convergence: a difference of 0.004 will not become meaningful in the remaining steps, and the card was better spent on the next arm | a maximum is genuinely not bilinear, so this was not another way of adding capacity -- and it changes nothing. One mechanism could still explain a null: a hard maximum passes gradient ONLY to the winning sub-vector, so with four modes each is trained about a quarter as often as a single 512-dimensional descriptor, and the form's extra expressiveness never gets paid for. `--mode-tau` now softens the maximum into a temperature-scaled logsumexp, which keeps the score non-bilinear while every mode learns from every pair; that arm is running at tau 0.1. If it also lands inside the noise floor, the scoring FORM is closed alongside capacity, depth and dimension, and what remains untried is a different adjacency SIGNAL rather than a different way of comparing the same one

M308 | The soft maximum over modes, and the closure of the scoring FORM | REJECTED / CLOSES THE FORM ALONGSIDE CAPACITY, DEPTH AND DIMENSION | M307's hard maximum tracked its control exactly, and the one mechanism that could still have explained a null was the sparse gradient -- a hard max trains only the winning sub-vector. `--mode-tau` softens it into a temperature-scaled logsumexp, keeping the score non-bilinear while every mode learns from every pair. tau 0.1, everything else identical | at step 2500: one mode (control) 0.2569 / 0.5951; four modes with a hard maximum 0.2449 / 0.5849; four modes softened 0.2476 / 0.5879. All three inside 0.012 of each other, against M306's noise floor of 0.028, and the hard arm also matched the control at 5000 (0.2804 against 0.2843) | the scoring form is not a lever. That was the last member of the family: capacity and depth (M197), dimension (M306) and now the form of the comparison itself all leave R@1 and R@20 where they were. Since a maximum is genuinely not bilinear, this is not another way of saying "more parameters do not help" -- it says the way the two descriptors are COMBINED does not matter either, which points the remaining fault at what the descriptors are computed FROM rather than at how they meet. Both mode runs stopped early; with three arms inside half the noise floor, convergence would not change the reading

M309 | Whether an independently initialised retriever is a new voter | REJECTED / CLOSES DIVERSITY, AND EXPLAINS M253's CEILING | M253 capped every selector at 525 correct edges of 1104 -- the union of top-1 over 18 scorers -- but those 18 are relatives, three architectures from one trunk on three views, and M267 showed a re-ranker cannot widen a union it does not propose into. `seam_embed_dim512` is the first retriever trained from scratch with its own initialisation, so its top-1 choices are its own. Mutual-best edge sets, 8 held-out boards, raw view, two orientations | alone the four are indistinguishable: v3 908 edges / 356 correct / 0.396, v2 896 / 346 / 0.390, local 909 / 355 / 0.395, dim512 892 / 345 / 0.390. Overlap of the TRUE edge sets is 0.877 between incumbents and 0.846 between them and the newcomer -- a difference of 0.03 on a Jaccard. Adding it to the union brings 12.4 new true edges for 138 new candidates, an increment at precision 0.09, and drops union precision 0.320 -> 0.297 | which edges a matcher gets right is decided by the DATA, not by the weights. An unrelated initialisation trained on the same corruption with the same objective agrees with the incumbents almost exactly as much as they agree with each other, so the roster is one voter measured four times and M253's 525 is a property of the task rather than of the roster. This closes ensembling as a route and, with it, the last model-side axis: capacity and depth (M197), dimension (M306), scoring form (M307, M308), re-ranking (M267), restored input (M292), calibration (M116), severity (M65) and now diversity all leave the edge sets where they are

M310 | Model limit or data limit: one clean board under eight independent corruptions | KEY / OVERTURNS THE CONTENT-AMBIGUITY READING / NAMES THE WALL EXACTLY | scratchpad/draw_union.py, 6 boards, seam_embed_v3, two orientations, mutual-best. M141 asked this of tile IDENTITY and never of the EDGES, which is what the cliff is counted in. One clean board, K independent draws of the generator -- fresh gain, offset, noise field and JPEG quality each time -- and the true edges pooled over draws | true edges in the union by draws pooled: 410, 497, 550, 587, 615, 637, 654, 669, still gaining 15 at the eighth and nowhere near saturating. True-edge Jaccard between two DRAWS 0.626; between two MODELS on one draw 0.877 (M309). Edges present in every one of eight draws: 125 to 294 depending on the board | the seams are READABLE and the noise field decides which ones we see this time. Two conclusions, opposite in sign. Against the programme: what limits us is the draw, not the architecture, so no model-side change can help and M309's interchangeable retrievers are explained -- four models looking at identical pixels see identical noise. For the programme: the failure is NOT intrinsic content ambiguity, since three independent looks already reach 550 and the union does not saturate, so nothing about flat sky against flat sky forbids the cliff. This retires the conclusion I was about to record -- that the matcher sits at a Bayes limit set by the content -- and replaces it with a sharper one: the limit is the single noise realisation we are given

M311 | A second look at the same seam, from different pixels | MECHANISM CONFIRMED, DOES NOT CONVERT | scratchpad/seam_segments.py, 8 boards. M310 says the noise field decides; we cannot draw it again but we can read different pixels, and the noise is independent per pixel. `--head local` emits one descriptor per row of the seam and the score already SUMS twenty per-row agreements, so sub-scorers come out of the shipping checkpoint with no training: score the top ten rows and the bottom ten as separate matchers | whole seam 505 edges / 227 correct / precision 0.447; rows 0-9 380 / 129 / 0.340; rows 10-19 376 / 125 / 0.333. TRUE-EDGE JACCARD BETWEEN THE TWO HALVES 0.214, against 0.626 for two noise draws and 0.877 for two trained models. Pooled: the two halves together 695 edges / 209 correct -- FEWER than the whole seam alone; whole plus halves 881 / 257; four quarters pooled 955 / 215; everything 1377 / 287 at precision 0.208 | the independence is real and is the largest this project has ever measured -- two readings of one seam that disagree four times as often as two separately trained networks -- and it does not pay, because half a seam is half the evidence. Each half finds a hundred and twenty-five true edges where the whole finds two hundred and twenty-seven, and their union still falls short of the whole. The marginal precision of everything the segments add is 0.07, the same rate as M309's independent model, for five times the volume. Diversity of INPUT PIXELS beats diversity of weights by volume and is equally useless at the operating point

M312 | Robust aggregation ACROSS the rows of a seam | REJECTED / THE SUM IS ALREADY RIGHT | scratchpad/row_aggregate.py, 12 boards. If the noise ruins rows locally (M311), summing all twenty lets a ruined row drag a true pair down as hard as a good row lifts it, and the median, a trimmed mean or the best k of twenty say instead "these edges line up wherever the noise let us see". Unlike M307's maximum over arbitrary descriptor sub-vectors, rows are a PHYSICAL decomposition -- a row is a place on the seam and the noise is independent per pixel | mutual edges / correct / precision / R@1: sum of all twenty (shipping) 483 / 205 / 0.419 / 0.240; best 15 425 / 196 / 0.455 / 0.234; best 12 391 / 187 / 0.474 / 0.226; best 10 363 / 178 / 0.485 / 0.217; best 6 306 / 152 / 0.491 / 0.191; median 421 / 164 / 0.387 / 0.202; trimmed 2 465 / 198 / 0.421 / 0.234 | every robust aggregator moves ALONG the existing precision-recall front rather than above it: precision rises to 0.491 and correct edges fall to 152, and R@1 declines monotonically as the aggregator gets more selective. Dropping rows is pruning, not denoising -- the sum is the optimal use of twenty noisy agreements when the noise is zero-mean, which it is

M313 | Segment agreement against the quantity that actually decides, the clean PREFIX | REJECTED / CLOSES THE SEGMENT THREAD | scratchpad/prefix_purity.py, 12 boards, the shipping harvest (three matchers, three views, two orientations, 13 of 18 votes). M270 is precise that the assembly needs not precision on average but a nearly clean FRONT -- 552 edges at 0.97 in the prefix, the same edges shuffled taking clean coverage from 0.931 to 0.268 -- and records that today's ordering carries 33 false edges in its first 200. Weak but INDEPENDENT evidence is what should purify a front, since the votes are eighteen readings of the same pixels and the halves are two readings of different ones | shipping sorted by votes: 208 edges, 171 correct, precision 0.817, longest front holding 0.97 = 50 edges. Halves breaking the ties: same set, front 59. Requiring one of two halves to agree: 168 edges at 0.849, front 35. Both halves: 63 at 0.901, front 35. Halves first and votes second: 250 at 0.657, front 35 | as a tie-break inside the vote ordering the independent evidence lengthens the clean front by nine edges, 50 to 59, and every use of it as a FILTER shortens the front while raising the average -- the same trade M254 found for every hand rule. The requirement is 552. A factor of nine is not a tuning gap, and this was the strongest independent signal available, so the selection thread closes with the front at 59

M314 | A perfect selector on the pool we actually have | KEY / RE-OPENS SELECTION / RETIRES THE 552 TARGET | scratchpad/pool_oracle.py, 12 boards, the shipping roster (three matchers, three views, two orientations). M268 priced the cliff with edges drawn UNIFORMLY from the true adjacency set, but our true edges are CLUSTERED -- a matcher succeeds where the photograph has texture -- and percolation depends on how edges are arranged, not only how many. So: keep every TRUE edge the pool proposes, which is the ceiling of every selector, and run the shipping placement on it. Control: the same count drawn uniformly, same boards. The frame prior is off in all three arms | the pool holds 432 true edges a board on average (236 to 611). PERFECT selection on it: largest clean component 194.0, clean coverage 0.682, fragment placement 0.4029. The same 432 edges drawn uniformly: largest 105.0, coverage 0.830, placement 0.2661. The shipping 13-vote harvest: 208 edges, 171 correct, largest 15.1, placement 0.0012 | clustering HELPS, by a factor of two on the largest component and 1.5 on placement, so M268's threshold of 552 is a property of uniform sampling and does not apply to us -- 432 clustered edges already deliver placement 0.40. And the number that matters: the evidence ALREADY IN HAND is worth placement 0.4029, against the 0.0012 the harvest extracts from it. The gap is a factor of 336 and it is entirely selection. Note the clean-coverage inversion in the control -- uniform edges score HIGHER coverage (0.830) with a SMALLER largest component (105), which is M270's warning made concrete: `is_clean` is all-or-nothing and stops measuring anything once components pass a few dozen fragments

M315 | The vote threshold swept and judged by PLACEMENT rather than clean coverage | REJECTED / THE METRIC WAS NOT THE PROBLEM | scratchpad/vote_placement.py, same 12 boards. M254 rejected every selector because clean coverage fell, and M270 warns that clean coverage stops meaning anything at large component sizes -- so the obvious suspicion after M314 is that selectors were tuned on a metric that lies in the regime we need | placement by threshold: 6 votes 0.0010 (701 edges, 309 correct), 8 votes 0.0016, 10 votes 0.0012, 11 votes 0.0009, 13 votes 0.0012 (shipping), 15 votes 0.0009, 18 votes 0.0056 (110 edges at precision 0.93). Perfect selection 0.4029 | placement is FLAT across the whole family at about 0.001, so it does not rank the thresholds differently from clean coverage and the metric was not what hid a better operating point. The correct reading is harsher and simpler: every real selector sits at 0.001 and the oracle at 0.40, so the transition happens entirely between precision 0.82 and 1.00 and nothing in the vote family reaches into it

M316 | How clean a selector must be, measured on the real pool | KEY / SETS THE TARGET THAT REPLACES 552 | scratchpad/precision_need.py, 12 boards. Take the pool's 432 true edges and admit false ones from the same pool to hit a chosen precision, with random weights so no ordering advantage is granted either way | placement, largest clean component: precision 1.00 -> 0.4005, 194.0; 0.99 -> 0.2092, 70.6; 0.98 -> 0.2254, 56.1; 0.97 -> 0.2115, 40.3; 0.95 -> 0.0622, 41.0; 0.90 -> 0.0369, 16.4; 0.82 -> 0.0084, 10.7 | the collapse sits between 0.97 and 0.95, confirming M269 and M270 on the real pool rather than overturning them, and the shape has one feature M269's uniform version did not show: the first FIVE false edges among 432 halve the largest component, 194 to 71, while placement only falls from 0.40 to 0.21 -- so the payoff degrades far more gracefully than the component statistics do, and 0.97 is worth 0.21 rather than nothing. THE TARGET, restated from measurement and replacing M305's R@1 0.52 and M268's 552 edges: about 430 edges at precision 0.97, all of them already inside the pool the shipping roster proposes. The best selector measured anywhere is M255's learned one at precision 0.943 on 113 edges, so the gap is four times the volume at somewhat higher precision -- a large ask, but a bounded and precisely defined one, and it needs no better matcher

M317 | The learned selector rebuilt against M316's target, with the independent feature | REJECTED / PER-EDGE SELECTION IS CLOSED | scratchpad/dump_seg.py, select_seg.py, select_placement.py. M255's selector rebuilt on SIXTY boards instead of twelve and given the one piece of evidence independent of the votes -- seam segment agreement, six near-independent readings of different pixels of the same seam (M311) -- as six features: raw half and quarter support, each end's share of it, and its rank among the rivals of the same fragment. LightGBM, 48 train boards, 12 held out, exclusive greedy decoding | precision at volume: 100 edges 0.951, 150 0.892, 200 0.833, 250 0.767, 300 0.707, 400 0.611, 430 0.586, 600 0.471. M255 on twelve boards read 0.878 at 150 and 0.810 at 200, so the rebuild is worth +0.014 to +0.023. FEATURE IMPORTANCE: the six competition features still lead (dst_share 13836, src_share 13151, dst_gap 11648, src_gap 11591, src_rivals 9207, dst_rivals 9054), then quart_share at 4697 -- ABOVE the rank-weighted score at 4574 and every raw vote count. PLACEMENT: top 100 0.0019, top 200 0.0025, top 300 0.0006, top 430 0.0014; the shipping harvest 0.0012; perfect selection on the same pool 0.4029 | the independent evidence is real and ranks above every vote count, exactly as M311 and M313 predicted it would, and it moves the operating point by two hundredths of precision, which is worth nothing where the payoff lives. M316 needs 430 edges at 0.97 and the best any selector reaches at that volume is 0.586. The reason is now visible in the shape rather than in any one number: precision falls smoothly from 0.951 at 100 edges to 0.586 at 430, so there is no threshold hiding a clean set -- the true edges past the first hundred are simply not separable from the false ones by any per-edge evidence this project can compute. What has NOT been tried is selection that is not per-edge: a search for a large mutually CONSISTENT set, where a subset's own geometric agreement stands in for evidence about its members

M318 | Consistency as a SEARCH over orderings rather than a cleanup pass | REJECTED / AND DIAGNOSES WHY THE FILTER LEAKS | scratchpad/ransac_consistent.py, 12 boards, the top 1200 candidates by the M317 selector. M270 ran the filter on one ordering; this samples many, weighted by the selector's probability through Gumbel noise at several temperatures, and keeps the accepted set carrying the most probability mass -- RANSAC on the assembly, with the probability as a sampling prior instead of as the decision | by probability, M270's own ordering: 537 kept at precision 0.503, largest component 141.8. Best of 240 samples at T=0.15: 545 at 0.503, largest 156.9; at T=0.4: 0.488; at T=1.0: 0.447. The 141.8-fragment component carries only 29.2 fragments at their true relative offset, 20.1%, and the best coherent block anywhere is 37.7 | searching over orderings buys nothing -- the deterministic order is already at the optimum of this objective -- and the reason the filter leaks is structural rather than a matter of ordering. Consistency can only reject an edge whose two ends already stand in a known relation, so an edge joining two fragments not yet connected to anything is accepted UNCHECKED, and most false edges are of that kind. The one number worth keeping: the best coherent block is 37.7 fragments against the shipping harvest's 15.1

M319 | Corroboration required for every merge | REJECTED / CANNOT BOOTSTRAP | scratchpad/corroborated_filter.py. M248 measured that a merge on one contact is right 11% of the time and on two independent contacts agreeing on the same offset 93.8%, and it starved as a stand-alone island merger at 1.3 to 2.3 fires a board. Inside the filter it is asked about every merge instead | zero edges kept, on every board | two singletons cannot corroborate each other: there is no second pair to ask, and at the start every fragment is a singleton. The rule has nothing to grow from

M320 | Corroboration demanded only where a false edge is expensive | ACCEPTED ON COVERAGE / REJECTED ON PLACEMENT | same script, and scratchpad/filter_placement.py for the payoff. A lone fragment joins on consistency alone; a merge of two islands of `floor` fragments or more must be backed by a second independent contact implying the same shift. M223 is the reason for the split -- damage scales with what the false edge fuses | coherent coverage, largest coherent block: consistency only 0.242 / 37.7; one contact to fuse islands of 2+ 0.494 / 27.2; of 3+ 0.413 / 31.7; of 5+ 0.359 / 32.6; two contacts to fuse islands of 3+ 0.491 / 22.7. Precision is flat at 0.503 to 0.514 throughout | the rule works on the quantity it was aimed at -- half the board lands in coherent blocks instead of a quarter -- and it works by FRAGMENTING, trading one large scrambled component for many medium coherent ones. Precision does not move, because the edges it rejects are as often true as false; what changes is where the damage lands

M321 | Every selection arm carried to placement, on one measure | REJECTED / CLOSES SELECTION / THE PAYOFF IS ONE LARGE COMPONENT | scratchpad/filter_placement.py, 12 boards, frame prior off in all arms so they are comparable to M314 and to each other. Every arm measured with `coherent`, the largest subset of a component agreeing on one board origin, because the 0.232 the harvest is usually quoted at was computed with all-or-nothing `is_clean` and M270 warns the two are not comparable | edges / precision / coherent coverage / largest coherent block / fragment placement. The shipping harvest 208 / 0.817 / 0.385 / 19.6 / 0.0022. Selector plus consistency 537 / 0.503 / 0.242 / 37.7 / 0.0029. Selector plus corroborated merges 470 / 0.514 / 0.494 / 27.2 / 0.0009. Perfect selection on the pool 432 / 1.000 / 0.682 / 194.0 / 0.4029 | coverage does not convert. Raising coherent coverage from 0.385 to 0.494 -- the largest such gain this project has produced -- moves placement from 0.0022 to 0.0009. The payoff column tracks the LARGEST COHERENT BLOCK and nothing else: 19.6, 37.7 and 27.2 all pay about the same nothing, and 194.0 pays 0.40. That is M263 restated from the other end, since placing many medium components against each other is precisely the coordination the objective cannot do, and it means the assembly is worth something only when one component is large enough to anchor the board by itself. With every per-edge, set-consistency and corroboration route measured, selection closes at a largest block of 38 against the 194 the same evidence would give a perfect selector

M322 | Spending the precision budget in ONE PLACE | MECHANISM CONFIRMED / SATURATES AT 38 | scratchpad/concentrate.py, 12 boards, the M317 selector's top 1200. M321 found placement tracks the LARGEST coherent block and nothing else, which makes M316's request -- 430 edges at 0.97 across the whole board -- the wrong shape: the most confident edges of a board are spread over the board by construction, so a hundred edges at precision 0.951 build fifty little blocks that pay nothing. Region growing asks a different question of the same evidence: seed on the best edge, then always take the most confident candidate that ATTACHES, starting a new block only when nothing does | at budget 100, confident prefix 50 components / largest coherent block 10.8 / precision 0.951; grown 1 component / block 30.4 / precision 0.733. At 150: 15.2 against 33.0. At 200: 20.1 against 35.3. At 300: 26.4 against 37.2. At 430: 35.9 against 37.5 | concentration nearly TRIPLES the only quantity that pays, at the same budget, and it costs precision because holding on to the block forces lower-ranked edges. Both rules converge near 37 by four hundred edges. This also explains M320 from the other side: demanding corroboration before islands fuse bought coverage by FRAGMENTING the board into 105 components, which is the opposite of what pays

M323 | Growing one block only while the evidence is confident | REJECTED / 38 IS A PROPERTY OF THE EVIDENCE | scratchpad/grow_floor.py, 12 boards, top 2500 candidates. M322 leaves the block growing past the point where the evidence supports it, so the missing knob is when to stop: extend only with candidates above a probability floor and let the block be as large as the evidence honestly allows | floor / edges / edge precision / block / coherent core / purity: 0.95 / 5 / 0.969 / 6.2 / 6.0 / 0.974; 0.90 / 7 / 0.970 / 7.7 / 7.4 / 0.972; 0.80 / 9 / 0.953 / 9.1 / 8.8 / 0.963; 0.60 / 18 / 0.919 / 17.6 / 15.5 / 0.897; 0.40 / 43 / 0.886 / 38.3 / 24.6 / 0.673; 0.20 / 173 / 0.706 / 156.2 / 31.6 / 0.233; 0.10 / 347 / 0.566 / 316.2 / 37.6 / 0.122 | the coherent core saturates at 37 to 38 whatever the floor, and below 0.4 the block grows only by absorbing fragments that do not belong -- 316 fragments carrying a core of 37. The same 37 to 38 appears in M318's consistency search, in M322's growth and here, from three unrelated procedures, so it is a property of the evidence rather than of any rule. Against it, the same pool under perfect selection carries a block of 194

M324 | Per-fragment NOISE LEVEL as a reliability signal, tested as an oracle first | REJECTED / IT IS THE REALISATION, NOT THE LEVEL | scratchpad/sigma_reliability.py, 12 boards. The generator draws sigma PER FRAGMENT, uniform on 40 to 55, and every matcher here ignores it -- `prep` subtracts a constant RING_SIGMA of 13.4 from every fragment alike. If the draw decides which seams are readable (M310), a quiet fragment should have readable seams and its edges should be trustworthy, which would be a per-edge feature independent of the score and the kind M317's selector has never had. Measured as an ORACLE, with the true sigma kept, because an estimator is only worth building if the quantity carries the signal | share of a fragment's true adjacencies recovered, by sigma quintile: 0.376, 0.360, 0.336, 0.344, 0.330. Candidate-edge precision by the LOUDER of the two fragments: 0.457, 0.412, 0.437, 0.422, 0.427. A cheap high-pass estimate of sigma correlates with the truth at 0.262 | across the generator's whole range the quietest fragments give up 0.376 of their adjacencies and the loudest 0.330, a relative difference of twelve percent, and by edge the signal is flat.
