So it is not the noise LEVEL that decides but the specific realisation, which is exactly M310's reading -- two draws at the same sigma already disagree on 37% of what they get right. Testing the oracle version first killed the idea in one run instead of after building an estimator for it

M325 | Filling a hole by UNANIMITY of its neighbours instead of by their summed cost | KEY / THE STEP M273 NEEDED / 0.996 WITH ORACLE CONTEXT | scratchpad/cell_consensus.py, 12 boards, 400 sampled cells each. Literature: percolation graph matching (Yartseva and Grossglauser, COSN 2013) never sums evidence -- it maps a pair once `r` already-mapped neighbours independently mark it, and the method turns on a sharp threshold in r. This project made the same distinction itself at island level in M248, one contact 0.111 against two independent contacts agreeing 0.938, and never asked it of a CELL. M273's every number ranks candidates by the SUM of seam costs over the revealed neighbours | summed, which is M273's measure: two neighbours 0.480, three 0.571, four 0.629. INDEPENDENT VOTES, each neighbour naming its own best free candidate -- fires on / precision when it fires: two of two 0.179 / 0.922; two of three 0.338 / 0.898; three of three 0.098 / 0.996; two of four 0.468 / 0.881; three of four 0.213 / 0.993; four of four 0.063 / 1.000 | agreement is a different object from strength, and the gap is the whole problem. M273 closed growth because 432 sequential decisions at 0.63 compound to nothing; at 0.993 they do not. The price is silence -- three-of-three speaks for one cell in ten -- so the question stops being accuracy and becomes whether the rule PERCOLATES

M326 | Why the rule grew nothing from a compact seed | PROCESS NOTE / A PROPERTY OF THE SEED, NOT THE RULE | scratchpad/percolate.py, first version. Bootstrap percolation from oracle seeds of 9, 38 and 144 fragments, laid out as a compact square | zero fragments grown, every seed size, both r=2 and r=3 | a solid rectangle is STABLE under the r=2 rule, and provably so: every empty cell beside a side of it touches exactly one placed cell, so a rule requiring two never speaks and the front never starts. This is the classical stability of a rectangle in bootstrap percolation and it was designed into the experiment by my choice of seed shape. Real coherent blocks are RAGGED, and ragged means concave, and concave is where two placed neighbours meet

M327 | The agreement front on ragged seeds, with and without nucleation | REJECTED / PRECISE AND STARVED, THE SHAPE EVERY GOOD RULE HERE TAKES | same script, seeds grown by a random walk. Two variants: pure, which places only where the required number of neighbours agree, and nucleated, which breaks a stall by placing the single most confident ONE-neighbour candidate to start a new row for the front to zip along | PURE, grown / correct / precision: seed 9 -> 1 / 1 / 0.500; seed 38 -> 4 / 3 / 0.888; seed 144 -> 8 / 7 / 0.871. Three-of-three pure grows nothing from any seed. NUCLEATED: seed 9 -> 448 / 28 / 0.063; seed 38 -> 408 / 41 / 0.101; seed 144 -> 338 / 72 / 0.214, filling 77 to 84 percent of the board | the pure rule keeps the 0.87 to 0.89 precision M325 promises and adds four to eight fragments before the front dies, because a ragged block has few concavities and each fill creates at most a couple more. The nucleated rule fills the board and is worthless: one wrong single-neighbour placement becomes context, the agreement rule then fires honestly on WRONG neighbours, and M263's collapse takes over -- 52.2% against true context and 0.8% against the search's own. Still, nucleated growth from 144 reaches 0.214 against M273's 0.054 on the same seed, four times better, which locates the remaining problem exactly: the second opinion has to come from somewhere other than the board's geometry, because geometry supplies it only at concavities and those are rare

M328 | Two matchers TRAINED on disjoint halves of the seam | REJECTED / CORRECTS M311 / INDEPENDENCE COMES FROM DIFFERENT SEAMS, NOT DIFFERENT PIXELS | src/seam_embed.py and train_seam_embed.py gained `--rows`; seam_embed_rowsA (rows 0-9) and rowsB (rows 10-19), same architecture as seam_embed_local to the last number, 12000 steps each. M311 sliced a descriptor trained to work as a SUM over twenty rows and measured true-edge agreement 0.214 between the halves -- four times more independent than two separately trained networks -- and concluded the idea was sound but the slices too weak. Training each half from the start tests that fairly. scratchpad/halves_trained.py, 12 boards | AS MATCHERS: rowsA R@1 0.2880 / R@20 0.6266, rowsB 0.2843 / 0.6215, against the whole seam's 0.3330 / 0.6661 on twice the evidence and twice the steps -- so a trained half is a full voter. Mutual edges / correct / precision: rows 0-9 864 / 309 / 0.359, rows 10-19 873 / 308 / 0.352, whole seam 874 / 335 / 0.382, v3 874 / 337 / 0.385. TRUE-EDGE JACCARD: the two trained halves 0.834; the whole seam against v3 0.884; two noise draws 0.626 (M310); the sliced halves 0.214 (M311). FILLING A CELL WITH ONE PLACED NEIGHBOUR, 4800 queries: rowsA alone 0.275, rowsB alone 0.272, whole seam alone 0.298; the two halves agreeing fires on 0.652 at precision 0.372; the whole seam and v3 agreeing fires on 0.763 at 0.366 | M311's independence was an artefact of the slices being WEAK, not of the pixels being different: strengthen each half and it converges on the same edges as everything else, 0.834 against the 0.884 of two ordinary networks. Reading different pixels of one seam is the same question asked twice. The consequence for M325 is exact and it is the useful part: a second GEOMETRIC neighbour lifts a cell from 0.298 to 0.922 because it is a different SEAM of the target fragment -- a different physical fact -- while a second view of the same seam lifts it to 0.372. Independence in this problem comes from different seams and nowhere else, which is why the agreement rule is precise and starved: a frontier cell has one neighbour, and only concavities have two

M329 | The agreement rule on a raster front, and why growth is closed by arithmetic | KEY NEGATIVE / CLOSES SEEDED GROWTH FROM THE MECHANISM RATHER THAN FROM A FAILED RUN | scratchpad/raster_grow.py, 12 boards, seed = the true top row and left column by oracle (47 fragments), 529 cells to fill. M327 left the agreement rule precise and starved on a blob front, and M328 ruled out a second opinion from re-reading the seam, so the remaining hope was the FRONT'S SHAPE: in reading order every cell arrives with its left and its upper neighbour already down, which would give the rule two independent voters everywhere instead of only at rare concavities | the summed rule (M273's) fills all 529 at precision 0.063 end to end, 0.322 on its first wave. The agreement rules, at two and at three, fill ZERO on every board, whether swept in reading order or run to a fixpoint | the raster argument is false and the reason is worth stating exactly. An L-shaped seed has exactly ONE cell with two placed neighbours, its inner corner; every other frontier cell has one. So the rule is offered a single candidate per round, and if it declines -- which at M325's firing rate it does four times in five -- no new two-neighbour cell is ever created and growth halts before it starts. Filling in reading order only produces two-neighbour cells if the cells before them were actually FILLED, which is what the rule refuses to do. THE ARITHMETIC THAT CLOSES IT: a growing front supplies at most two neighbours, because a third only appears at a hole BEHIND the front, and two neighbours cap the step at 0.922 (M325). A block of 194 needs about 150 dependent steps, and 0.922^150 is 5e-6. Three neighbours would give 0.996 and 0.996^150 is 0.55 -- live -- but no front ever offers three. Seeded growth is therefore closed not by any particular search but by the geometry of a frontier, which agrees with M265's decay from an oracle seed and M273's 5.4% and now explains both

M330 | Ordering ONE row, given its membership by oracle | KEY NEGATIVE / PRICES THE PEEL'S BASE CASE PROPERLY / THE COST, NOT THE SEARCH | the user proposed assembling ring by ring, outermost first, and verifying a ring against the one inside it -- two rings must agree along their whole shared boundary, 84 independent tests of one hypothesis. M250 called the base case unreachable by an ARGUMENT, "23 consecutive correct right edges at 0.72", which prices a greedy chain and nothing else. scratchpad/row_solve.py, 12 boards: the true top row handed over as a SET, shuffled to defeat the identity leak, then ordered by greedy chaining and by beam search of width 3000 | R@1 against all 576 fragments 0.304; R@1 restricted to the row's own 24 candidates 0.457. Pairs recovered: greedy chain 0.449, beam search 0.431. The beam recovers the row exactly on 0 of 12 boards. THE TRUE ORDER IS THE CHEAPEST ON 0 OF 12 BOARDS, and the beam's best path scores 30.55 BELOW the truth | two findings, and the second is the one that closes it. Knowing which fragments share a row is worth a real amount -- the seam query rises from 0.304 to 0.457 when the candidate list shrinks from 575 rivals to 23 -- so the user's compression is genuine. But the ordering objective is BROKEN at this scale: the true row is never the minimum-cost Hamiltonian path, and a stronger search finds better-scoring wrong orders, which is why the beam does worse than greedy. M250's conclusion stands and its reason does not: the base case fails not because a chain of 23 decisions is long but because the correct chain does not win even when membership is given. This is M273's lesson on the user's scenario -- when global optimisation loses to greedy, the cost surface is what fails

M331 | Does matching the NEXT ring make the true order win | REJECTED / THE OBJECTIVE RANKS WELL AND ITS OPTIMUM IS STILL WRONG / AND A REUSABLE BOUND ON GROUPING | the user's second point: a ring is verified against the ring inside it, so two rows carry 23 + 23 horizontal seams and 24 vertical ones between them -- seventy constraints on a hypothesis instead of twenty-three. Tested at its strongest: both rows handed over as oracle SETS and shuffled, row 1's best response to a candidate row 0 solved EXACTLY by linear assignment, every arm scored on all seventy terms. scratchpad/two_rows.py, 12 boards, beam width 3000, 200 random orders a board as the population | the row alone, true cost minus the beam's best: +30.55, so the truth is dearer. With the ring-to-ring terms added: +42.59, DEARER STILL. The truth beats a random order under the joint objective on 1.000 of 2400 comparisons. Row 1's best response beneath the TRUE row 0 places 0.462 of its cells and 0.330 of its adjacent pairs | three readings. Adding constraints does not rescue the optimum -- tripling them widens the gap rather than closing it, so the failure is not a shortage of evidence about the hypothesis. The objective nonetheless separates truth from noise perfectly, which places it exactly where M243 and M245 put the placement objective: honest, and nearly flat at the top, where wrong answers beat it. And even granted a perfect previous ring and oracle membership, the next ring lands 46% of its cells, so the peel would lose half of each layer. THE BOUND WORTH KEEPING, from M330 and this together: shrinking a fragment's candidate list from 575 rivals to 23 -- a 24-fold compression, and more than any grouping by ring, colour or semantics could deliver -- lifts the seam query only from 0.304 to 0.457. Any future idea of the form "restrict the candidates by some class" is capped there

M332 | Six aggregation forms for the ordering objective | REJECTED / THE RAW SUM IS THE BEST OF THEM / CLOSES THE FORM | the user's inference after M330 and M331: if the truth is never the optimum, change the objective. Every objective this project optimises is a SUM OF RAW SEAM COSTS, and M310 suggests why that could be the wrong shape -- a seam destroyed by this draw of the noise is expensive although CORRECT, and a sum has no defence against such outliers. scratchpad/objectives.py, 12 boards, the oracle top row, beam 3000. Raw; truncated at the 25th and 10th percentile so a destroyed seam is capped; the partner's rank in the source's own list; a hinge that charges nothing for the top three; per-row z-scores; and the negative log of a per-row softmax -- the last three being COMPETITION, which M255 found dominates every vote count as a feature | truth minus the beam's best, in units of the entry spread, and pairs recovered: raw 4.66 / 0.431; truncated q25 16.89 / 0.435; truncated q10 18.79 / 0.424; rank 8.79 / 0.428; hinge 20.79 / 0.275; z-scored 5.71 / 0.420; log-softmax 5.70 / 0.428. The truth is optimal on 0 of 12 boards under every form except the hinge, which manages 0.083 while recovering the fewest pairs | the outlier hypothesis is WRONG and its refutation is the finding: truncating makes matters worse, not better, so what beats the truth is not a handful of expensive honest seams but a supply of cheap accidental ones, and capping the penalties only makes those easier to collect. The raw sum turns out to be the best of the six and every form lands at the same 0.43 pairs. The aggregation is not the lever: the objective fails because of what it is made OF

M333 | The seam quality at which the true row becomes the optimum | KEY / PRICES THE PEEL THE WAY M305 PRICED THE PIPELINE | scratchpad/objective_gate.py, 12 boards. M332 leaves the question as a quantity rather than a form, so the cost matrix is blended with an oracle one -- zero on true pairs, one elsewhere -- each blend standing for a matcher of some quality whose in-row R@1 is MEASURED rather than assumed. Generous by construction, since an oracle's errors are absent rather than rarer, so the gate it reports is a lower bound | in-row R@1 / truth is optimal / pairs right / row recovered exactly: 0.457 (as delivered) / 0.000 / 0.438 / 0.000; 0.826 / 0.333 / 0.848 / 0.250; 0.957 / 0.833 / 0.960 / 0.500; 0.993 / 0.917 / 0.989 / 0.833; 1.000 / 1.000 / 1.000 / 1.000 | THE GATE IS ABOUT 0.95 IN-ROW R@1 and we are at 0.457. The transition is sharp -- even at 0.826 the row comes out whole only a quarter of the time -- which is the same percolation shape M268 found for edges, here on a single row. And the number is already generous twice over: it is measured AFTER the user's 24-fold compression, which lifted the query from 0.304, and against an oracle whose errors do not exist. Together with M331's bound on grouping, the peel is priced: membership is worth +0.15 R@1, the peel needs +0.50 more, and no aggregation form supplies any of it

M334 | How fast a self-check catches a wrong placement | KEY / THE CHECK IS SHARP AND SILENT | the user proposed assembling by trial, cycle verification and backtracking, accepting that a run may be slow because a month of compute on several machines is available. The proposal avoids the trap of M266's beam, whose branches were ranked by the objective that cannot tell them apart, by deciding on a CHECK instead of a score. scratchpad/verifier_power.py, 12 boards, 6000 trials: two true neighbours of a cell revealed by oracle, a fragment placed there -- the true one in one arm, the best WRONG candidate in the other -- and M325's agreement check then run one cell further along | after a CORRECT placement the check fires on 0.160 of cells and is right 0.936 of the time; after a WRONG one it fires on 0.021 and is right 0.438. A ratio of 7.49. Under the two-neighbour cost the true fragment is top-1 0.506 of the time at mean rank 30.3 of 576 | the check is sharp -- a wrong placement survives it only one time in fifty -- and it is SILENT, since it declines to speak about 84% of correct placements too. Silence is therefore not evidence, which is why M327 starves when it treats silence as rejection. What the ratio supports is not per-step rejection but ACCUMULATION over a window

M335 | Confirmations accumulated over a window, and why they verify but do not guide | KEY NEGATIVE / PRICES THE BACKTRACKING SEARCH | scratchpad/confirm_window.py, 12 boards, raster growth from the true top row and left column. A branch whose every placement is TRUE against the real greedy branch, counting confirmations over windows of 30, 100 and 300 cells. The first version of this compared a clean start against a poisoned one and was degenerate -- the clean arm poisons itself by step two, placements right 0.206 -- so both arms were wrong branches and identical | confirmations, correct branch against greedy: window 30, 3.9 against 2.2, separation 0.71 sd; window 100, 14.7 against 6.2, 1.86 sd; window 300, 67.0 against 15.9, 5.61 sd. The correct branch's firing RATE RISES with the window, 0.131 to 0.147 to 0.223, as cells acquire more neighbours, while the greedy branch's falls, 0.072 to 0.062 to 0.053 | the check VERIFIES and does not GUIDE, and the distinction is the whole result. A long correct assembly is separated from a wrong one by 5.6 standard deviations, which vindicates the intuition that two rings joined correctly announce themselves. But that separation is measured against an ORACLE branch, and no search can produce one: after a correct first choice the lookahead still degrades immediately, 0.206 of placements right at thirty cells and 0.066 at three hundred, exactly as it does after a wrong first choice. The difference between the two lives only in the handful of cells around the first placement -- M334's 0.160 against 0.021 on about three cells, some 0.4 confirmations against a Poisson spread of 2.2, about 0.27 sd -- while choosing among twenty candidates needs roughly three. So the branching factor never collapses, 20^300 stands, and no amount of hardware reaches it. Verification without a way to REACH the candidate is not a search

M336 | Weeding components by their own agreement, on components large enough to test | SIGNAL CONFIRMED AND STRENGTHENED / STILL NO CONVERSION / M211's BLOCKER IS GONE AND A NEW ONE REPLACES IT | the user proposed verifying the assembler's chains, discarding the sickest and re-assembling. M206 accepted that as a CHOOSER (self-consistency picks a correct core on 91.7% of boards against 58.3% by size) and M211 rejected it as a FILTER for a reason that has since expired -- "the harvest is made almost entirely of two- and three-tile components, and those are self-consistent by arithmetic". Today's filters build much larger ones, and M334 supplies a sharper health test than M206's summed form: in-component neighbours voting INDEPENDENTLY and having to agree. scratchpad/comp_health.py, 12 boards, 175 components of six tiles or more | correlation of health with a component's coherent fraction +0.310, against M206's +0.226 for the summed form. Correlation of SIZE with the coherent fraction -0.134, confirming M206's warning that larger components are likelier to be corrupt. Choosing the core by health gives a coherent fraction of 0.836 against 0.629 by size. AS A FILTER: every component -> placement 0.0012, coverage 0.416, largest coherent block 27.8; dropping the sickest half -> 0.0022, 0.273, 18.8; keeping only components with any confirmation at all changes nothing, since none scores zero | the health signal is real and is the best truth-free component score this project has, and M211's objection no longer applies -- there is plenty of large material now. The filter still does not convert, and the new reason is the one every route has hit today: dropping components SHRINKS THE LARGEST COHERENT BLOCK, 27.8 to 18.8, and M321 measured that placement tracks that block and nothing else. Weeding buys purity with exactly the quantity that pays. The chooser remains the usable half, and it is now measured at 0.836 against 0.629 -- but it needs a consumer, and the only one it ever had, growth from a trusted seed, is closed by M329

M322-CORRECTION | PRIORITY: M322 is a re-derivation of M222, and it overwrote its script | PROCESS NOTE | M222, "Concentrating the edge budget instead of spreading it", REJECTED, ran from `scratchpad/concentrate.py`. Today's M322 was written to the SAME PATH and destroyed it. The ninth accidental re-derivation in this project and the first to lose an artefact | M222: strict seeds then a lower vote bar for edges touching the build, "seed 15 grow 10 gives 283 edges at precision 0.743, clean 0.090, largest 9.0" against a flat ten-vote threshold's 332 at 0.688, clean 0.123, largest 9.0 -- every seeded variant at or below its flat counterpart. Its closing line states what M323 then re-measured: "concentration would need a criterion that stays selective as the build grows, and adjacency to the build is not one" | what M322 adds over M222 is genuinely new and does not excuse the rest: M222 grew by LOWERING A VOTE BAR for edges touching the build, M322 grows by attaching in order of a learned selector's probability, and it is scored on the largest COHERENT block -- the quantity M321 identified as the only one that pays and which M222 could not have used. On that measure concentration triples the block, 10.8 to 30.4. But the saturation M323 found at 37 to 38 is M222's closing sentence measured again. The grep rule exists for exactly this and I ran it for the ring idea, for synchronisation and for the hole filler, and not for this one

M337 | Is the objective WRONG or merely DEGENERATE | KEY DIAGNOSTIC / IT IS WRONG, AND THAT DECIDES THE REMEDY | scratchpad/degenerate.py, 12 boards. M330 to M333 leave the true row costing about 30 units more than the beam's best, which with z-scored costs and 23 terms is 1.3 sd per pair -- pairs that look BETTER than true adjacency. Two readings call for opposite remedies. Wrong: the cheap pairs are unrelated fragments that happen to look continuous, and the objective must be replaced. Degenerate: they are INTERCHANGEABLE fragments, since any photograph carries many near-duplicate patches of sky or grass, and then the truth merely sits on a plateau, the remedy is a tie-break, and the loss is far smaller than placement accuracy suggests because swapping look-alikes costs little SSIM. Separated by rendering the row from the CORRUPTED fragments in each order and scoring it against the CLEAN target strip | SSIM against the clean target: the TRUE order 0.4724, the beam's best 0.2052, a random order 0.1483. The beam recovers 0.057 of the 0.324 available, eighteen percent. The clean fragment the beam misplaces differs from the one that belongs there by 40.2 grey levels against 53.5 for two fragments of the row picked at random | the objective is WRONG. Interchangeability is real but small -- the beam's swaps are a quarter more alike than random, not duplicates -- and its output looks far closer to a shuffle than to the answer. So no tie-break rescues it and a different objective is required, which is what the user asked for. The same measurement names the target: SSIM against the clean image separates the two orders completely, 0.4724 against 0.2052, so what is needed is a stand-in for that judgement which does not see the answer -- a score for "this assembly looks like a photograph" rather than "these seams match". The bar is low, since the seam cost currently ranks the beam's order ABOVE the truth, and any global score that ranks them the right way round is strictly better

M338 | A JOINT scorer over a cell's whole neighbourhood, trained 576-way | REJECTED / THE SUM IS STILL BETTER | src/train_hole_query.py, new. Every scorer here is a function of two fragments, and M204's neighbourhood numbers come from SUMMING calibrated seam log-probabilities, which cannot see that the continuations must agree at the corners where three fragments meet. `train_hole_filler.py` was written for this and never run, as a cross-encoder whose 576-way objective is unaffordable -- so it defaulted to seven negatives, which is precisely M107's diagnosed failure ("the failure was budget, not architecture... inheriting the trunk removes the rediscovery"). Rebuilt as two towers: keys are the frozen retriever's four descriptors, already paid for with 17000 steps of 576-way training, and the query is one convolutional pass over the whole 3x3 neighbourhood with a blank centre, so the objective is 576-way at one forward per hole | top-1 among 576 by neighbours known, at 12000 steps: 1 -> 0.1276, 2 -> 0.3464, 3 -> 0.4766, 4 -> 0.5208, flat from step 11000. M204 by summing: 0.324, 0.511, 0.608, 0.669 | the joint query loses at every neighbour count, and the shape of the loss says it is doing the intended thing without doing it well enough: it reaches 39% of the summed score at one neighbour and 78% at four, so the joint context is worth something and the gap closes as context grows -- but it never overtakes, and it plateaus. The honest caveat is budget again, the query encoder being fresh where the keys are not; the honest reading is that a single pass over the neighbourhood does not out-argue four separately calibrated seam opinions

M339 | A GLOBAL learned objective, and why none can have its optimum at the truth | KEY NEGATIVE / CLOSES THE OBJECTIVE FAMILY BY A PROPERTY OF THE DATA | scratchpad/strip_energy.py. M337 asked for a stand-in for the SSIM judgement that never sees the answer: a score for "this assembly looks like a photograph". Trained as a discriminator its optimum sits on real photographs by construction, which is the property every objective so far lacks. A convolutional energy over the whole 20x480 strip, trained on 900 boards with true rows as positives and rows perturbed by one to twenty-four transpositions as negatives, then tested on BEAM orders it never saw | the seam cost ranks the true row above the beam's on 0 of 12 boards, which is the known starting point. THE ENERGY MODEL ALSO RANKS IT ABOVE ON 0 OF 12, preferring the beam by a mean margin of 7.47. Every board individually | a global learned image prior lands exactly where the pairwise cost lands, and the reason is a fact about the data rather than about either judge. Put the three numbers together: SSIM against the clean image says the true row is right, 0.4724 against 0.2052 (M337), while BOTH judges -- local and global, hand-made and learned -- say the beam's row is smoother and more photograph-like. So the true arrangement is not the most plausible arrangement of these corrupted fragments; at sigma 40 to 55 the true seams are noisy and a wrong order finds joins that look better than real ones. THE CONSEQUENCE, and it answers the request that prompted it: an objective whose optimum is the truth cannot be built on this data, because the observation itself supports the wrong answer. Any score that rewards plausibility will prefer the same forgery. Caveat stated plainly: the negatives here were easy transpositions, so this is not the strongest discriminator obtainable -- but the margin is 7.47 on 12 boards of 12, and the seam cost reaches the same verdict with no training at all

M340 | An energy trained against its OWN optimum | ACCEPTED / M339's CONCLUSION RETRACTED / FIRST OBJECTIVE EVER TO PREFER THE TRUTH | scratchpad/hard_energy.py, same architecture and evaluation as M339, one thing changed: the negatives. M339 used random transpositions and concluded that no plausibility objective can prefer the truth. That outran the experiment -- a discriminator shown only shuffles learns to find the discontinuity, and the beam's order is built to have none, so the test showed a discontinuity detector failing against something optimised against discontinuity. The idea the easy negatives hid: the beam's order is not merely smooth but TOO smooth, since a real photograph carries a characteristic amount of discontinuity at a seam, so being too smooth must cost as much as being too rough -- and a model can only learn that if shown negatives that are too smooth. Negatives here are beam orders under the seam cost, the current objective's own optimum, diversified with Gumbel noise; one in four stays a shuffle so the easy signal is not lost. 240 training boards, held-out evaluation at beam width 3000 | ranks the true row above the beam's: the seam cost 0.000, the energy on easy negatives 0.000 (M339), THE ENERGY TRAINED AGAINST ITS OWN OPTIMUM 0.583, seven boards of twelve. The mean margin moves from -7.467 to -0.214, a factor of thirty-five | RETRACTION: M339 concluded "an objective whose optimum is the truth cannot be built on this data, because the observation itself supports the wrong answer". That is wrong and this measurement refutes it. The observation supports the truth well enough to be found, provided the judge is trained against the forgeries it will actually meet rather than against easy ones. This is the first objective in the project that ranks the truth above the arrangement the seam cost prefers, on any board at all. It is not yet enough -- 0.583 with the mean margin still slightly negative -- but the mechanism works and the recipe for improving it is standard: regenerate the negatives under the NEW energy and repeat, whose fixed point is an energy whose minimum sits on the data

M341 | Contrastive ROUNDS, and the practical number | REJECTED AS RUN / THE DIRECTION SURVIVES, THE IMPLEMENTATION DOES NOT | scratchpad/cd_rounds.py. M340 left the recipe: regenerate negatives under the current energy and repeat, whose fixed point is an energy whose minimum sits on the data. Round one draws negatives from a pool of noisy beam orders; later rounds pick, per board, the candidate the CURRENT energy likes best and push the energy up there. Added here is the number that decides -- generate-and-rerank, since in deployment the truth is not among the candidates: draw eight orders, pick by the energy, and measure the pick | truth ranked above the seam cost's pick / pairs recovered by the energy's pick / by the seam cost's pick / SSIM of each: round 1 0.333 / 0.391 / 0.413 / 0.237 against 0.213; round 2 0.333 / 0.409 / 0.413 / 0.196 against 0.213; round 3 0.167 / 0.373 / 0.413 / 0.228 against 0.213. Round three's training loss reaches 0.0000. The true order scores SSIM 0.472 | the rounds make it WORSE, and the loss going to zero says why: with 240 boards the energy separates the training pool perfectly and has memorised it rather than learned what distinguishes a true row. Generate-and-rerank does not convert either -- the energy's pick recovers 0.373 to 0.409 of the pairs against the seam cost's 0.413, and the SSIMs are a wash. HONEST NOTE ON POWER: M340 measured 0.583 and round one here measures 0.333 on the same twelve boards, under two standard deviations apart, so part of M340's margin was noise. What survives is the sign: the energy ranks the truth first sometimes and the seam cost never does, 0.000 on every board. The direction stands and this implementation does not, with a diagnosis that names its own remedy -- 240 training boards and 12 evaluation boards are both too few

M342 | How the perfect selector's placement is DISTRIBUTED over boards | KEY / THE MEAN DESCRIBES NO BOARD / AND IT SPLITS THE FAILURE IN TWO | scratchpad/bimodal.py, 24 boards, the same oracle-on-pool arm as M314 with the frame prior off. M314 reported a mean of 0.4029 over twelve boards; the rendered sheet showed one board of three essentially solved and two at zero, which a mean cannot express | mean placement 0.2455 over 24 boards against M314's 0.4029 over 12 -- the project's own rule that twelve boards settle nothing, again. THE DISTRIBUTION: 29.2% of boards place more than half their fragments, up to 0.8368; 37.5% place more than a fifth; 54.2% place essentially nothing; the MEDIAN is 0.0069. Correlation of true edges in the pool with placement +0.543 | the mean describes no board: nine of twenty-four are nearly solved and thirteen are at zero, with almost nothing between. And the largest-component column splits the thirteen failures into two kinds that need opposite remedies. Some have no large coherent block and genuinely did not percolate. But several carry a large one and still score zero -- 321 fragments coherent at placement 0.0104, and 215, 180, 145, 136, 133 likewise -- which is not an assembly failure at all but an ORIGIN failure: the block is internally right and standing in the wrong place. About a quarter of all boards are of that second kind, and the mechanism for fixing it exists and was switched OFF in this measurement for comparability with M314 -- the frame prior of M247, which is the project's only absolute cue

M343 | The frame prior against the ORIGIN failures M342 found | ACCEPTED / IT MOVES THE MEDIAN FROM ZERO TO 0.45 / AND IT IS NOT FREE | scratchpad/origin_rescue.py, the same 24 boards and the same oracle-on-pool arm, run twice: with the frame prior off, as M314 and M342 had it, and with it on at weight 1.0, which is the shipping default. M342 split the thirteen zero boards in two -- some never percolated, others carried a large coherent block standing in the wrong place, which is an ORIGIN failure and not an assembly failure | boards placing more than half: 0.292 without, 0.417 with. Boards at essentially zero: 0.542 without, 0.250 with. Mean placement 0.2455 against 0.3860, and the MEDIAN 0.0069 against 0.4531. Of the thirteen boards at zero, the frame lifts SIX above 0.2, and the rescues are exactly the ones M342 predicted from the largest-component column: 321 coherent fragments 0.0104 -> 0.7413, 180 fragments 0.0000 -> 0.7135, 215 -> 0.6528, 145 -> 0.5851, 199 -> 0.4983, 136 -> 0.4531. IT ALSO BREAKS TWO: 224 coherent at 0.3941 -> 0.0069, and 149 at 0.5243 -> 0.0000 | the median moving from 0.0069 to 0.4531 is the shape of the result: with perfect selection and the origin fixed, more than half of all boards place nearly half their fragments, where before the median board placed nothing. M342's diagnosis is confirmed by the identity of the rescued boards rather than by the average. The two losses matter as much as the six gains -- a false border reading drags a correct assembly off its place -- so the prior is a per-board bet rather than a free improvement, which is the second reason after M342 to choose per board instead of globally. Note for reading every earlier entry: M314, M316, M321, M336 and M342 all ran with the prior OFF for comparability, so each understates the oracle ceiling

M344 | The REAL pipeline per board with the frame prior, and an empirical hardness model | KEY / THE BIMODALITY IS THE ORACLE'S, NOT OURS / AND VOLUME PREDICTS THE OUTCOME AT 0.955 | scratchpad/hardness.py, 24 boards. Two gaps: every real-pipeline number measured on 2026-08-24 ran with the frame prior OFF for comparability with M314, and M343 then showed the prior moves the ORACLE median from 0.0069 to 0.4531. And M342's bimodality poses the algorithm-selection question, whose standard answer is SATzilla's empirical hardness model -- cheap instance features, a learned difficulty prediction, a per-instance choice. Eleven truth-free features here: harvest volume at three vote thresholds, mean votes, component count and largest, the top-three size share, M336's self-consistency health, the matcher's median margin, and the border detector's confidence | THE PIPELINE: placement mean 0.0104, median 0.0017, max 0.1128; adjacency mean 0.216, median 0.216, max 0.339; SSIM mean 0.0994. Boards above placement 0.05: 8.3%. Above 0.2: NONE. FEATURE CORRELATIONS with the assembly's adjacency: voted edges at 13 votes 0.955, mean votes 0.933, at 10 votes 0.925, at 18 votes 0.909, largest component 0.750, median matcher margin -0.728, top-three share 0.424, component count 0.286, health -0.231. The harvest's true edge PRECISION, which needs the answer, correlates only 0.652 | two findings of opposite sign. The bimodality belongs to the ORACLE arm and not to ours: our pipeline is uniformly bad, no board reaching a fifth of its fragments, so the per-board policy M342 and M343 pointed at has no consumer yet -- there are no solved boards to tell apart. And the hardness model works far better than expected: the NUMBER of voted edges predicts the assembly's adjacency at 0.955, and does so better than the harvest's true precision at 0.652, so what decides a board's fate is the VOLUME of agreed evidence rather than its purity. That is directly actionable, because the vote threshold is GLOBAL at 13 of 18 while the volume it yields varies board to board, and M288 and M289 swept that threshold only globally

M345 | A vote threshold chosen PER BOARD, to a target volume | ACCEPTED, PENDING ITS CONTROL | scratchpad/adaptive_votes.py, 24 boards, frame prior on, annealed. M344 found the edge COUNT predicts the assembly's adjacency at 0.955 while the harvest's true precision manages 0.652, so volume decides and purity follows -- and the bar that sets the volume is global at thirteen of eighteen while the count clearing it varies board to board by a factor of several. Each board's bar is lowered until its harvest reaches a target, swept around the volume the fixed bar delivers | edges / precision / adjacency / placement / SSIM. Fixed thirteen: 212 / 0.813 / 0.216 / 0.0104 / 0.0994. Per board at 150: 163 / 0.857 / 0.187 / 0.0063 / 0.0964. At 200: 214 / 0.798 / 0.211 / 0.0103 / 0.1010. At 260: 279 / 0.725 / 0.232 / 0.0092 / 0.1037. At 320: 345 / 0.658 / 0.244 / 0.0100 / 0.1055. At 400: 441 / 0.577 / 0.250 / 0.0145 / 0.1050 | more edges at lower precision win monotonically until about 400, confirming M344's reading from the other side. But the best arm runs at a MEAN bar of 8.2 and eight is the pipeline's own default, so the gain may be the lower bar rather than the per-board choice, and M288 and M289 already swept the bar globally. Not claimable without a matched control

M346 | The matched control: is it adaptivity or just a lower bar | ACCEPTED / IT IS THE ADAPTIVITY / SHIPPED | scratchpad/adaptive_control.py, same 24 boards, fixed bars beside per-board targets so the arms can be read at equal volume | fixed 13: 212 edges, adjacency 0.216, placed 0.0104, SSIM 0.0994. Fixed 10: 333, 0.242, 0.0069, 0.1026. Fixed 9: 380, 0.248, 0.0062, 0.1021. Fixed 8 (the pipeline's default): 431, 0.244, 0.0021, 0.0983. Fixed 7: 500, 0.230, 0.0059, 0.1013. PER BOARD at 320: 345, 0.244, 0.0100, 0.1055. At 400: 441, 0.250, 0.0145, 0.1050. At 500: 569, 0.233, 0.0051, 0.1028 | at matched volume the per-board choice wins. Against fixed eight, which yields 431 edges to the per-board target's 441, SSIM goes 0.0983 to 0.1050 and fragment placement 0.0021 to 0.0145; against fixed nine at 380 edges the same holds. Adjacency is a wash across the middle of the range, 0.244 to 0.250, so the gain shows in what is built from the edges rather than in the edges themselves. HONESTY ON SIZE: 24 boards, and the placement figures are 1.2 against 8.4 fragments of 576, small and noisy -- SSIM is the reliable column, averaged over 24 boards of 230k pixels, and it gives +0.0056 over the fixed thirteen and +0.0067 over the shipping default. Wired into src/harvest_votes.py as `votes_for_target` and exposed as `--vote-target`, default 400

M347 | The per-board bar through the WHOLE pipeline | RETRACTS M346's SHIPPING CLAIM / THE ADJACENCY GAIN DOES NOT CONVERT | `src/infer_composed.py --validate 24`, run twice, once with `--vote-target 400` and once with the fixed bar at thirteen, everything else identical: annealed placement, frame prior 1.0, the restorers, the seam levelling, NLM, the bilateral and the colour field. M346 claimed the per-board bar on the strength of a scratchpad harness; this is the same question asked of the shipping path | fixed thirteen: adjacency 0.218, place_acc 0.0106, SSIM 0.2711 at alpha 1.0, 0.3126 at 0.5, 0.3545 at 0.15. Per board at 400: adjacency 0.245, place_acc 0.0054, SSIM 0.2635 / 0.3095 / 0.3538. Flat fill 0.3525 in both | the adjacency gain is real and reproduces -- 0.218 to 0.245, matching the harness's 0.216 to 0.250 -- and it does NOT convert: SSIM falls at every alpha, by 0.0076 at full texture. Placement flips sign between the two measurements, 0.0145 against 0.0021 in the harness and 0.0054 against 0.0106 here, which at 3 to 8 fragments of 576 is noise either way. WHY THE HARNESS LIED, and it is a lesson worth keeping: it weighted edges by VOTE COUNT while the pipeline weights them by the minimum margin, so `build_directed_components` built different components from the same edge set. The fixed-bar arm reproduced exactly, which is what made the difference look real. The default is reverted to the fixed bar and `--vote-target` stays as an option, since the adjacency gain may convert under a different renderer. GENERAL RULE, paid for here: a harness that reproduces the CONTROL is not thereby measuring the pipeline; only the arm's own numbers can show that, and they must be compared through the shipping path before anything is called shipped

M348 | The vote bar and the edge ORDER, swept through the whole pipeline | ACCEPTED: THE DEFAULT MOVES TO TEN / ORDER IS CLOSED | `infer_composed.py --validate 24`, six arms, everything through the shipping path -- annealed placement, frame prior 1.0, restorers, seam levelling, NLM, bilateral, colour field. Two questions at once. The ORDER in which components are built has never been swept although the weight passed to `build_directed_components` does nothing else: it sorts, and M270 measured that ordering takes clean coverage from 0.931 to 0.268. And the bar itself has never been swept through THIS path, with the frame prior and annealing in place | adjacency / placement / SSIM at alpha 1.00, 0.50, 0.15. Margin order at 13 votes (control): 0.218 / 0.0106 / 0.2711 / 0.3126 / 0.3545. VOTE order at 13: 0.215 / 0.0099 / 0.2719 / 0.3129 / 0.3545. Votes-then-margin at 13: 0.217 / 0.0103 / 0.2709 / 0.3128 / 0.3547. Margin at 10 votes: 0.242 / 0.0113 / 0.2701 / 0.3134 / 0.3551. Margin at 8, the previous default: 0.240 / 0.0012 / 0.2626 / 0.3084 / 0.3536. Per-board target 400: 0.245 / 0.0054 / 0.2635 / 0.3095 / 0.3538 | ORDER IS CLOSED and the reason is worth keeping: at thirteen votes the harvest is 212 edges over 576 fragments, so edges almost never conflict and nearly every one is accepted whatever the sort -- ordering can only matter where the builder has to refuse something. TEN VOTES is the optimum and the pipeline's default was EIGHT, the worst of the three: ten beats it on every column, most sharply on placement, 0.0012 to 0.0113. Against thirteen it trades 0.001 of SSIM at full texture for 0.024 of adjacency and a little placement, which the project's own rule -- rank by the assembly, not by SSIM -- settles in ten's favour. Default changed

M349 | Hygiene: every never-validated default, swept through the shipping path | THREE DEFAULTS CONFIRMED, TWO MECHANISMS CLOSED, ONE CANDIDATE | `infer_composed.py --validate 24` from the new baseline of ten votes. Several options were set from measurements taken in configurations that no longer exist, and two carried defaults contradicting each other -- `--corroboration` is 0.0 on the command line and 4.0 inside `solve_layout` | adjacency / placement / SSIM at 1.00, 0.50, 0.15. Baseline: 0.242 / 0.0113 / 0.2701 / 0.3134 / 0.3551. Corroboration 4.0: 0.242 / 0.0109 / 0.2697 / 0.3132 / 0.3552. Content 0.5: 0.240 / 0.0104 / 0.2701 / 0.3133 / 0.3549. Frame 0.2: 0.241 / 0.0108 / 0.2687 / 0.3122 / 0.3545. Descent: 0.241 / 0.0103 / 0.2690 / 0.3128 / 0.3548. Fill by field: 0.229 / 0.0111 / 0.2724 / 0.3152 / 0.3558. Votes 9: 0.244 / 0.0016 / 0.2643. Votes 11: 0.240 / 0.0109 / 0.2705 | three defaults confirmed where they stand: the frame prior at 1.0 beats 0.2 by 0.0014, annealing beats descent by 0.0011, and ten votes is a sharp optimum -- nine collapses placement sevenfold, to 0.0016. Two mechanisms close END TO END: M248's corroboration is worth -0.0004 and the trained content border detector 0.0000, both confirming from the shipping path that they are starved rather than wrong. THE CANDIDATE is filling leftover cells by the colour field rather than by seam, worth +0.0023 at full texture and positive at every alpha, with adjacency falling because a colour fill does not preserve index adjacency. It contradicts M226 -- "how the leftover cells are filled is worth NOTHING", spread one ten-thousandth across four rules including random -- but M226 ran with NO FIELD anywhere, so its fill supplied the colour while this one only places texture under a field that already supplies it. Different regime, and at 0.0023 against an inter-arm noise of about 0.001 it is not claimable on 24 boards; re-running both arms on 48

M350 | The leftover fill, on 48 boards | ACCEPTED / DEFAULT CHANGED / AND IT NARROWS M226 RATHER THAN OVERTURNING IT | `infer_composed.py --validate 48`, both fills, everything else at the new baseline: ten votes, annealed, frame prior 1.0. M349 saw +0.0023 at 24 boards against an inter-arm noise near 0.001, which is not enough to move a default, and it contradicts M226's "how the leftover cells are filled is worth NOTHING" | 48 boards, fill by seam: adjacency 0.244, placement 0.0069, SSIM 0.2646 / 0.3089 / 0.3525. Fill by FIELD: 0.230 / 0.0066 / 0.2673 / 0.3111 / 0.3532. The deltas are +0.0027 / +0.0022 / +0.0007, against +0.0023 / +0.0018 / +0.0007 on the 24-board set | the deltas reproduce on a doubled board set almost exactly, so this is real rather than noise, and the default moves to the field fill. It costs adjacency, 0.244 to 0.230, and gains score anyway, which is the clearest single illustration of why this project ranks by assembly AND keeps SSIM as a control: a colour fill does not preserve index adjacency but does put roughly the right colour in roughly the right place. M226 is NARROWED, not overturned -- it measured with no field anywhere, so its fill had to supply the colour itself and every rule was equally bad at that; here the field supplies the colour and the fill only places texture beneath it, which is a different question and has a different answer

M351 | The margin, the post-processing and the high-pass cut-off, through the shipping path | MARGIN CLOSED / POST-PROCESSING ALL EARNS ITS PLACE / SIGMA IS MIS-SET | `infer_composed.py --validate 24` from the baseline of ten votes and the field fill. Three groups: `--margin`, which M224 measured at edge precision 0.997 for eight votes at margin 2.0 against 0.942 for votes alone and which has never been run end to end; the three post-processing stages, each chosen in its own era; and the high-pass cut-off, which M182 set to 20 as one fragment wide | baseline: adjacency 0.229, placement 0.0111, SSIM 0.2724 / 0.3152 / 0.3558. Margin 0.5: adjacency 0.146, SSIM 0.2692. Margin 1.0: adjacency 0.095, 0.2717. Margin 2.0: adjacency 0.046, 0.2703. No seam levelling: 0.2431. No NLM: 0.2487. No bilateral: 0.2665. Sigma 12: 0.2779 / 0.3177 / 0.3564. Sigma 30: 0.2681 | THE MARGIN IS CLOSED and it confirms M344 from a second mechanism: it buys purity by spending volume, adjacency falling 0.229 to 0.046, and SSIM does not rise -- exactly what "volume decides, purity follows" predicts. M224 was right on its own metric, edge precision, and that metric does not reach the output. THE POST-PROCESSING all pays: seam levelling is worth 0.029 of SSIM, NLM 0.024, the bilateral 0.006, none of them redundant. AND SIGMA IS MIS-SET: 12 beats 20 by 0.0055 at full texture and wins at every alpha, while 30 loses. Note that adjacency and placement are identical across this whole group, 0.229 and 0.0111, since sigma touches only the render -- so the gain is clean. Sweeping 5 to 16 for the optimum

M352 | CORRECTION to M351: sigma is not a free knob, it is the detail trade again | PROCESS NOTE / AND IT PRICES A BETTER EXCHANGE RATE | scratchpad sweep of `--sigma` from 5 to 30 through `--validate 24`. M351 read sigma 12 beating 20 by 0.0055 and called the gain clean because adjacency and placement are identical across the group. That reading was wrong in substance: sigma touches only the render, but what it changes there is the VISIBLE DETAIL, which is the quantity the user's standing rule protects | SSIM at alpha 1.0 and the 12-pixel high-pass detail beside it: sigma 5 -> 0.2940 at detail 14.8; 7 -> 0.2861 at 17.7; 9 -> 0.2817 at 19.6; 11 -> 0.2790 at 20.9; 12 -> 0.2779 at 21.4; 14 -> 0.2761 at 22.1; 16 -> 0.2747 at 22.7; 20 -> 0.2724 at 23.3; 30 -> 0.2681 at 23.8. The clean target's own detail is 34.6 (M145) | the curve is monotone in both columns: every step down in sigma buys score by showing less texture, which is the same bargain as alpha and is exactly what the standing rule forbids taking silently. So sigma 12 is NOT shippable as a quiet default change and M351's claim is corrected here. What the sweep does establish is an EXCHANGE RATE, and it is three times better than alpha's: sigma trades 0.0024 to 0.0029 of SSIM per unit of detail surrendered where M145 measured alpha at 0.0066. That makes a legitimate question out of an illegitimate one -- not "how much smoothness can we buy" but "at the SAME visible detail, is a small sigma with a raised alpha better than a large sigma at alpha 1.0". Testing sigma 5 and 9 against alphas up to 2.8, which restores the detail the smaller cut-off gives up

M353 | Small sigma with a raised alpha, at MATCHED visible detail | REJECTED / M182's CHOICE STANDS / AND THE EXCHANGE RATE WAS AN ILLUSION | `infer_composed.py --validate 24`, sigma 5 and 9 against alphas from 1.0 to 2.8, compared against the shipping point of sigma 20 at alpha 1.0, which renders SSIM 0.2724 at 12-pixel high-pass detail 23.3. M352 measured sigma trading 0.0024 to 0.0029 of SSIM per unit of detail against alpha's 0.0066 and asked the only legitimate question that follows: at the SAME visible detail, does a small cut-off with a raised alpha beat a large one | every arm reaching the reference detail or more scores below it. Sigma 5 at alpha 1.8: 0.2596 at detail 25.4. Sigma 9 at 1.4: 0.2588 at 26.8. Sigma 5 at 2.2: 0.2466 at 30.1. Sigma 9 at 1.8: 0.2401 at 33.2. Sigma 5 at 2.8: 0.2303 at 36.0. The only arms scoring above the reference show LESS detail than it -- sigma 5 at alpha 1.4 reaches 0.2750 at detail 20.3 | at matched detail the current default wins outright, so the sigma default does not move and M182's choice of one fragment width is vindicated in the configuration that exists now. The exchange rate of M352 was an illusion because it is not a constant: a 5-pixel high-pass keeps only the FINEST scale, which in these fragments is mostly noise, so raising alpha there amplifies noise, while a 20-pixel cut-off keeps mid-scale structure that is far more often right. The rate depends on WHICH scale of texture is being shown, and cheap-looking rates at small sigma buy the worst detail available. Caught before shipping

M354 | The user finds a solved board by eye, and M344's reading is corrected | KEY / THE REAL PIPELINE IS BIMODAL AFTER ALL / AND A TRUTH-FREE DETECTOR THAT WORKS | the user picked img_000916 out of the shipped honest_v5 submission as nearly solved -- "MOBILDATA ENGINEERING" and "100 000" readable across several fragments, a person whole. Two detectors were then built and tested. scratchpad/compare_subs.py, where_edges.py, test_hardness.py | BLOCKINESS, the comb of energy at the fragment frequency, ranked img_000916 FIRST of 700 -- and against ground truth on 32 boards it correlates with placement at +0.323 in the WRONG direction, with adjacency at +0.051 and with SSIM at -0.046. Its least-blocky third has placement 0.0019 against the most-blocky third's 0.0167. REJECTED. THE SUM OF THE THREE LARGEST COMPONENTS, truth-free and taken before the render, ranks img_000916 NINTH of 700; its top five look assembled by eye where the median and the worst are mush; and only 10 boards of 700 reach the user's level | M344's conclusion is corrected: solvable boards exist, they are about 1.4% of the test set, and twenty-four held-out boards could not contain one. Blockiness failed for a reason worth keeping -- a smooth board has no comb whether it is assembled or not, so on boards that are all unsolved it measures smoothness alone, and the user's board is both smooth and coherent, which made the two coincide by accident. The working detector uses the assembly's own structure rather than the picture

M355 | A per-board vote bar chosen by that detector | REJECTED BY CONSTRUCTION / AND IT PRICES THE HEADROOM | scratchpad/per_board_policy.py, 32 held-out boards, the harvest taken at bars 8, 10 and 13 with SSIM read from the renders `infer_composed --dump-validate` produced, so no harness stands between the policy and the output | fixed bar 8: adjacency 0.231, placement 0.0014, SSIM 0.2607. ORACLE choosing the best bar per board: 0.2741; oracle by placement: 0.0143 | the policy fails by MY construction, not by the feature. top3 grows mechanically with the number of edges, so a lower bar always yields larger components and "take the largest top3" degenerates to "always take the lowest bar" -- which is exactly what its numbers show. The feature compares BOARDS at a fixed bar, where it was validated, and cannot compare bars on one board. THE HEADROOM IS REAL AND LARGER THAN ANYTHING SHIPPED TODAY: choosing the best bar per board is worth 0.2741 against 0.2661 for the best fixed one, +0.008, where the two default changes of M348 and M350 together were worth about +0.010. What it needs is a feature comparable ACROSS configurations -- a property of the finished LAYOUT rather than of the edge count

M356 | Four judges of the finished LAYOUT, including an unbiased one | ALL REJECTED / THE PER-BOARD OPPORTUNITY IS GATED BY THE SAME DOOR AS EVERYTHING ELSE / AND IT GIVES M340 A CONSUMER | scratchpad/layout_judge.py, 32 held-out boards, the harvest at bars 8, 10 and 13 with the LAYOUT itself saved by `infer_composed --dump-validate`, so the judge scores exactly the arrangement that produced the dumped render. Fixed 8: 0.2607 at 0.19. Fixed 10: 0.2660 at 0.31. Fixed 13: 0.2661 at 0.50. Per board by mean seam: 0.2609 at 0.16. By trimmed seam: 0.2616 at 0.22. By mutual share: 0.2607 at 0.19. BY THE HELD-OUT MATCHER: 0.2610 at 0.22. Oracle: 0.2741. Chance is 0.33 | every judge lands below the best CONSTANT, and every hit rate is at or under chance, so holding a fixed bar beats choosing by any of them. The held-out matcher fails with the rest, which locates the fault precisely: it is not bias from sharing the roster but the nature of the quantity, since all four reward seams that MATCH and M330 to M339 established that a wrong arrangement often matches better than the true one. So the per-board opportunity is real -- 0.2741 against 0.2661, larger than both of today's shipped changes together -- and it is locked behind the same door as everything else. The one key measured all day is M340's: an energy trained against its OWN optimum ranks the truth above the forgery on 0.33 to 0.58 of boards where a seam cost manages 0.000. That thread was left at overfitting on 240 boards with no consumer; it now has one, and it is the only judge with any demonstrated ability to prefer the truth

M357 | The board described once, the bar chosen from the description | REJECTED / AND IT EXPLAINS WHY EVERY PER-BOARD POLICY HAS FAILED | scratchpad/descriptor_policy.py, the same 32 held-out boards and the three saved layouts. The user judges honest_v6 worse than v5 by eye and the reports name the difference: v5 ran the bar at 13, v6 at 10, and rendering both fills at the same bar shows them INDISTINGUISHABLE, so M226 is confirmed for appearance even where M350 measured the field fill worth 0.0027 of SSIM. What the user sees is the purity-volume trade -- 212 edges at precision 0.813 against 333 at 0.678. M355's feature is valid as a description of a BOARD at a fixed bar even though it cannot compare bars, so it is measured once at bar 13 and used to choose | boards with a rich harvest do best at bar 10 (0.2561 against 0.2539 at 13) and thin boards do best at 13 (0.2800 against 0.2772), which is the REVERSE of the hypothesis. Correlation of the descriptor with which bar wins: -0.002. Threshold policies give 0.2656, 0.2648 and 0.2660 against 0.2661 for a fixed bar of 13 | no policy beats a constant, and the same table explains why every attempt has failed. Read the absolute numbers: thin-harvest boards score 0.2758 to 0.2800 and rich ones 0.2473 to 0.2561, so a board's SSIM is set mainly by its own TEXTURE -- smooth boards sit nearer the flat fill and score higher whatever we do with them. Per-board selection is therefore trying to predict a difference of 0.008 against a board-to-board spread of 0.03, and that ratio, not the absence of a feature, is what defeats it. The consequence for the live thread is exact: a judge must rank ARRANGEMENTS OF ONE BOARD, where texture is held constant, which is how M340's energy was posed and why it alone showed signal

M358 | Why every per-board policy failed, and the tension it uncovers | KEY / DIAGNOSIS / CLOSES THE PER-BOARD THREAD AND NAMES THE PROJECT'S REAL DILEMMA | analysis of the three saved layout sets over 32 held-out boards, decomposing each target into what a per-board policy could WIN (the spread across configurations within one board) against what it must see through (the spread across boards) | SSIM: within-board 0.0184, across-board 0.0806, ratio 0.23; oracle gain +0.0079. ADJACENCY: within 0.0533, across 0.0472, RATIO 1.13; oracle gain +0.0085. Placement: within 0.0139, across 0.0195, ratio 0.72. And the fixed bars themselves: bar 8 gives adjacency 0.231 at SSIM 0.2607, bar 10 gives 0.229 at 0.2660, bar 13 gives 0.188 at 0.2661 | first, why M355, M356 and M357 all failed: aimed at SSIM they were predicting a difference four times smaller than the board-to-board spread they had to see through, and no feature survives that ratio. Aimed at ADJACENCY the ratio inverts to 1.13 and the problem becomes learnable in principle. But the second reading makes that hollow and is the more important of the two: MORE correct adjacency comes with LOWER SSIM, consistently across all three bars. The mechanism is mechanical -- a lower bar puts more fragments into components, the annealer places a component as a UNIT, and a large component in the wrong place costs more SSIM than the same fragments scattered, while a higher bar leaves more of them to a fill that places by COLOUR, which SSIM rewards. So the metric pays for guessing colour and the eye pays for guessing texture, and at our level of assembly they pull apart. The standing rule chooses the eye, which makes bar 8 the honest setting and bar 13 the scoring one

M359 | Dissolving the components we do not trust, instead of placing them as blocks | REJECTED | src/infer_composed.py gained `component_health` and `--dissolve`; a component of six fragments or more that does not confirm itself is not placed as a unit and its fragments go to the fill. M358 measured the mechanism this rests on: the annealer places a component as a UNIT, so a large one in the wrong place costs more SSIM than the same fragments scattered, while the fill places by colour, which SSIM rewards. M336's health is the trust signal, which chooses a correct core 0.836 of the time against 0.629 by size and which M336 could not convert as an edge filter -- this is a different consumer for it. Swept through `--validate 32` | threshold / adjacency / placement / SSIM at alpha 1.0: off 0.229 / 0.0092 / 0.2660; 0.3 0.226 / 0.0089 / 0.2652; 0.5 0.210 / 0.0099 / 0.2661; 0.7 0.129 / 0.0033 / 0.2608; 0.9 0.076 / 0.0023 / 0.2626 | at the only threshold that does not damage the assembly, SSIM does not move at all -- 0.2661 against 0.2660 -- while adjacency falls from 0.229 to 0.210, and above it everything collapses together. M358's mechanism is real and dissolution does not reach it, which says the health signal does not separate the MISPLACED components from the merely weak ones: it measures whether a component agrees with itself, and a component can be internally perfect and standing in the wrong place, which is exactly the origin failure M342 found in a quarter of boards. The knob stays, off by default

M360 | The annealer has never annealed | KEY / A CENTRAL COMPONENT MEASURED AS A NO-OP | scratchpad seed sweep and direct calls to `place_search.anneal`. M357 pointed here: choosing between CONFIGURATIONS per board is hopeless because board texture swamps the difference, while restarts of one configuration differ only in the arrangement. `--seed` was exposed and five restarts run over 32 boards | the five restarts return BITWISE IDENTICAL layouts on every board -- 1.00 distinct layouts of 5 -- so the spread in SSIM, adjacency and placement is exactly 0.0000. Calling `anneal` directly with seeds 0 and 1 and any mix of move types returns the same board. The seed IS wired: `rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)` | the annealing phase of `anneal` does nothing at all, and its output is always the deterministic greedy initialisation that precedes it. Twenty thousand iterations a board are spent for no effect. This also corrects every comparison of `--place anneal` against `--place descent` made in this project, M349's included: they compared a GREEDY INITIALISATION against coordinate descent, and the annealing M245 introduced was never in the pipeline

M361 | Why: 93% of proposals do not fit, and NONE of the rest is uphill | KEY / THE MOVE CLASS IS WRONG, NOT THE TEMPERATURE | `place_search.anneal` instrumented with counters over 20000 iterations on a 60-component board | proposals / unfit / uphill / accepted / improved. Uniform relocation: 20000 / 18693 / 0 / 0 / 0. Half local displacements: 20000 / 17765 / 0 / 3 / 0. Ninety-five per cent local: 20000 / 17036 / 0 / 1 / 0. After the greedy initialisation the board is 43 to 72 per cent occupied | two separate faults, and the second is the real one. A uniform relocation must find a free rectangle the size of the component's bounding box, which at this occupancy fails 93% of the time; making the moves LOCAL fixes that only partly, to 85%. But of the thousands of proposals that DO fit, not a single one is uphill in any configuration. So the greedy initialisation is a deep local optimum for the move class "relocate one component" -- greedy places each component at its best position given those before it, and moving one alone can only break its own contacts. Locality is not the issue; ARITY is. A swap of two components was added, being the smallest move that trades one component's contacts for another's, and it does produce uphill moves where single moves produce none: 16, 9 and 16 on three boards, improving the record 3, 0 and 6 times. The adjacency it buys is 0.324 -> 0.324, 0.277 -> 0.277 and 0.177 -> 0.178

M362 | A depth-TWO harvest, through the shipping path | REJECTED / CLOSES M253's WAY OUT | src/harvest_votes.py gained `_depth` and a `depth` argument; `infer_composed.py` gained `--depth`. M253 measured the constraint every selector since has run against: the union of every scorer's top-1 holds 525 correct edges of 1104 while percolation needs 552, so no rule on top-1 evidence can reach it, and it named the way out -- the union of every top-2 holds 599. M254 and M255 built SELECTORS on that pool and could not extract more than about 160 edges, but the shipping harvest has always taken mutual-best only and has never been run on the deeper pool, and M344 measured that what decides a board is the VOLUME of agreed evidence. Swept over the vote bar, since a deeper candidate list makes votes easier to come by | matched control, depth 1 at 10 votes with current defaults: adjacency 0.229, placement 0.0092, 69.4 components, SSIM 0.2660. Depth 2 at 13 votes: 0.174 / 0.0014 / 60.3 / 0.2599. At 15: 0.191 / 0.0033 / 70.8 / 0.2617. At 17: 0.185 / 0.0035 / 78.2 / 0.2650. At 18: 0.175 / 0.0037 / 76.9 / 0.2635 | the deeper pool loses at every bar and on every column. The extra true edges arrive with far more false ones and the vote cannot separate them -- which is the same failure M254 and M255 found for hand-written and learned selectors on this pool, now confirmed for the vote, since a vote is a selector too. So M253's way out is closed from the third direction: the evidence to cross percolation exists in the top-2 pool and nothing this project can build extracts it. Volume decides only when the volume is AGREED volume, which is what M344 actually measured

M363 | What the eighteen scorers are actually made of | KEY / INDEPENDENCE COMES FROM THE INPUT, NOT THE WEIGHTS / MEASURED AT MATCHED COUNT | the shipping roster is 3 architectures x 3 views x 2 orientations and was accepted as one object (M212, M214, M237); its three factors have never been separated. `infer_composed.py --validate 32` with `--restorers none` newly permitted, the vote bar held at 55% of whatever the roster size is | 2 scorers, one architecture on the raw view: adjacency 0.168, placement 0.0022, 31.1 components, SSIM 0.2613. Thirty-six, four orientations: 0.224 / 0.0084 / 69.5 / 0.2668 | at the SAME number of votes, three views beat three architectures by 0.035 of adjacency and by 61 components against 38 -- half again as much coherent structure from the same budget. Doubling the roster with orientations buys nothing at all. So independence of opinion in this problem comes from showing the matcher a different VERSION OF THE PIXELS, not from different weights reading the same ones, which is M309's edge-level reading confirmed end to end with a matched count and which explains both of today's failures to manufacture independence from weights -- a from-scratch retriever (M309) and half-seam matchers (M328), each agreeing with the incumbents at 0.83 to 0.85. It also explains why orientations are flat: a symmetry shows the SAME pixels to different heads, which is a variation of weights. PRACTICAL RULE: grow the roster by views, never by architectures or orientations

M364 | Five views instead of three | REJECTED UNDER THE STANDING RULE / IT IS THE SAME TRADE AGAIN | two more restorers exist in the checkpoint directory, `tile_restorer_big` and `tile_restorer_seam`, giving five views and thirty scorers. It also refutes M121 in this regime, which held that at near-zero placement the whole SSIM gain comes from adjacency: here SSIM rises while adjacency falls

M365 | Where the adjacency went, and what the views are really worth | KEY / MY COMPARISON WAS THE FAULT / AND THE VIEWS ARE VERY UNEQUAL | scratchpad diagnostic of the harvest itself, then `--vote-target` used to compare rosters at MATCHED HARVEST VOLUME rather than at a matched fraction of votes.
