AND THE BARS WERE NOT MATCHED: 10 of 18 harvests 346 edges at precision 0.682 while 17 of 30 harvests 211 at 0.810, so "the same fraction" was in fact a much higher bar. AT MATCHED VOLUME: at 250 edges, three views give adjacency 0.209 / placement 0.0072 / SSIM 0.2641 and five give 0.203 / 0.0103 / 0.2658; at 350 edges, three give 0.233 / 0.0077 / 0.2658 and five give 0.225 / 0.0047 / 0.2622 | the trade M364 reported was an artefact of my comparison: matching the FRACTION of votes across rosters of different size does not match the selectivity, because weak views rarely agree with strong ones and their presence raises the effective bar. At matched volume the extra views add no correct edges at all -- adjacency is lower at both volumes. So M363's axis is right and the available material is not: views are where independence lives, but a view worth adding has to be comparable in quality to the ones already there, and these two are less than half as good. The general lesson for roster comparisons: match the harvest VOLUME, never the vote fraction

M366 | ANALYTIC views beat every learned restorer as voters | KEY / THE MATERIAL M365 SAID WAS MISSING / FIRST ARM TODAY TO IMPROVE THE ASSEMBLY | scratchpad/analytic_views.py, then wired into `infer_composed.py` as `--analytic`. M363 put independence in the INPUT and M365 found the available inputs too weak -- the learned restorers score solo mutual-edge precision 0.148 to 0.280 against the raw fragments' 0.396, and at matched harvest volume they add no correct edges. But a view is any transform of the pixels, and a filter costs nothing | SOLO PRECISION, one architecture, two orientations, 8 boards: raw 0.396; median 3 0.352; non-local means h=6 0.346; bilateral 7/50 0.342; unsharp 0.324; gaussian 1.0 0.291; tile_restorer 0.280; nlm h=12 0.253; tile_restorer_mgc 0.221; tile_restorer_big 0.164; tile_restorer_seam 0.148. END TO END at matched volume of 350 edges, 3 architectures: raw plus median, nlm and bilateral (24 scorers) gives adjacency 0.241, placement 0.0106, SSIM 0.2620 -- against the same-volume control on raw plus two restorers (18 scorers) at 0.233 / 0.0077 / 0.2658, and the shipping baseline at 0.229 / 0.0092 / 0.2660 | three free filters make better voters than every restorer this project has trained, and the mechanism is the one M292 saw from the other side: a restorer is trained to RECONSTRUCT and invents detail doing it, which the matcher then believes, while a filter can only remove and stays nearer the distribution the matcher was trained on. End to end the analytic roster gives the best adjacency measured today, 0.241, and better placement than the shipping baseline, 0.0106 against 0.0092, for 0.004 of SSIM. Under the standing rule -- rank by the assembly, keep SSIM as a control -- this is the first arm today that IMPROVES the assembly instead of trading it away

M367 | Confidence-weighted voting, estimated without labels | ACCEPTED / PARTLY CLOSES THE ADJACENCY GAP | src/harvest_votes.py gained `scorer_weights`: build a consensus from the plain vote, then weight each scorer by the share of ITS edges the consensus holds -- one round of the unlabelled scheme of Parisi et al. (arXiv 1303.3257), motivated by M365's finding that the views run from 0.483 to 0.173 in solo precision while each casts an equal vote | the unlabelled weight correlates with true solo precision at +0.899 and orders the five views correctly: raw 1.199, tile_restorer 1.133, mgc 1.052, big 0.847, seam 0.768, against true precisions 0.557, 0.396, 0.325, 0.261, 0.223. END TO END on the 30-scorer roster: at bar 14 weighted gives adjacency 0.220 / placement 0.0084 / SSIM 0.2660 against unweighted 0.209 / 0.0073 / 0.2645; at bar 17, 0.193 / 0.0109 / 0.2680 against 0.181 / 0.0110 / 0.2695 | weighting recovers 0.011 to 0.012 of the adjacency that heterogeneous views cost, at both bars, and gains SSIM at the lower one. The estimate needs no labels and no held-out data, so it can weight any future roster. It does not by itself make the weak restorer views worth having -- M365 settles that at matched volume -- but it is the right way to combine views of unequal quality, which is what a roster grown by views will always be

M368 | The teammate's idea: contours as adjacency evidence | REJECTED AT FRAGMENT SCALE / AND THE PICTURE SHOWS WHY | the user's teammate proposed using image contours, with a picture of a whole board's edge map surviving the noise. Tested two ways: Canny maps fed to the trained matcher as a VIEW, as M366 fed the filters, and contour continuity as a seam COST needing no network -- gradient magnitude and orientation matched row by row across the join, weighted by edge strength so flat rows contribute nothing. scratchpad/contour_views.py, 8 boards | AS A VIEW: raw control 0.396; Canny after a bilateral 0.049; after a median 0.044; after non-local means 0.025. AS A COST: 0.067, 0.065, 0.064 for the three denoisings, against the analytic MGC's 0.085 (M276) and the learned matcher's 0.396 | contours lose on both routes and the reason is scale, not noise. M369 measures the survival separately and it is fine; what fails is that a 20x20 fragment contains no LINES, only texture, and Canny cannot tell one from the other -- rendered at 8x, the edge map of even the most edge-rich fragment is a picket fence, on the CLEAN fragment as much as the corrupted one. Binarising also throws away the gradient magnitude and all colour, which is most of the signal at that scale, and the matcher already reads both. By M365's rule this is closed twice over: a view worth adding must be comparable to those present, and this one is eight times weaker than the raw view

M369 | Do contours survive the corruption, and at what scale | DIAGNOSTIC / THE TEAMMATE IS RIGHT ABOUT SURVIVAL / THE SCALE IS THE PROBLEM | scratchpad/contour_survival.py, 8 boards. Canny on the clean fragment against Canny on the corrupted one, as an edge F1 with a one-pixel tolerance, measured at fragment scale and again on the whole assembled board | fragment scale: F1 0.483 raw, 0.502 after a bilateral, 0.515 after non-local means. BOARD scale: 0.592, 0.694, 0.651. Edge density 0.117 to 0.140 throughout | contours do survive -- two thirds of the edge pixels of a 480x480 board are still there after the corruption, which is exactly what the teammate's picture shows -- and they survive markedly better at board scale than at fragment scale. So the failure of M368 is not that the noise destroys contours. It is that the object which survives is a LONG line, and the matcher's decisions are made where no long line exists

M370 | Contours as a JUDGE of the assembled board | FIRST TRUTH-FREE JUDGE WITH REAL SIGNAL / DOES NOT YET CONVERT | the teammate's second idea, and it dodges M368's objection by using contours where M369 says they live. M356 tried four truth-free judges of a finished layout and every one landed at or below chance, because all four reward seams that MATCH and M330 to M339 established a wrong arrangement often matches better. A contour judge asks instead whether a LINE crosses the seam. Every statistic is normalised by the same quantity measured inside the fragments, so board texture -- the confound that made M354's blockiness detector track smoothness -- cannot leak in. scratchpad/contour_judge.py, the 32 held-out boards and the three saved layouts each | picking the higher-ADJACENCY layout of one board, chance 0.333: mean contour chain length 0.625, seam crossing share 0.562, normalised crossing rate 0.531, long-chain share 0.500, edge density 0.469. As a POLICY choosing the vote bar: adjacency 0.230, placement 0.0040, SSIM 0.2633, against 0.231 for a fixed bar of 8 and an oracle-by-adjacency of 0.239 | mean contour chain length picks the better layout 0.625 of the time against a chance of 0.333 -- the first truth-free judge in this project to beat chance at all, where M356's four including a held-out matcher did not. The mechanism is the one the teammate proposed: a correct assembly carries its lines through the seams and a wrong one truncates them every twenty pixels. It does not convert, and the arithmetic says why rather than the judge: the oracle's whole margin over the best constant is 0.008 of adjacency, and a 0.625 picker cannot extract it. So this is a foothold rather than a gain -- and it is the right FEATURE for the one judge that has ever preferred the truth, M340's energy trained against its own optimum, which was built on pixels and had no structural feature to read

M371 | The analytic roster shipped: filters replace the restorers as views | ACCEPTED / DEFAULT CHANGED / FIRST CHANGE IN TWO DAYS THAT IMPROVES THE ASSEMBLY WITHOUT TRADING ANYTHING | the chain is M363 (independence lives in the INPUT: three views beat three architectures 61.2 components to 38.3 at matched votes), M365 (the learned restorers are too weak to add, solo precision 0.148 to 0.280 against the raw view's 0.396, and rosters must be compared at matched harvest VOLUME) and M366 (free filters beat every restorer: median 0.352, non-local means 0.346, bilateral 0.342). `infer_composed.py --validate 48` | AT MATCHED VOLUME 350, 48 boards. Base, raw plus two restorers: adjacency 0.234, placement 0.0056, SSIM 0.2671. Raw plus median and bilateral: 0.242 / 0.0106 / 0.2676. Raw plus three filters: 0.241 / 0.0077 / 0.2642. AT THE OLD FIXED BAR of 10 votes the analytic roster LOSES -- 0.224 / 0.0027 / 0.2612 against 0.230 / 0.0066 / 0.2673 -- and the component count says why: 54.5 against 70.0, because analytic views agree with each other less than restorer views do and fewer edges reach ten votes. NEW DEFAULTS END TO END, passing nothing but place, frame and alpha: 0.242 / 0.0106 / 68.1 components / 0.2676, against the previous defaults' 0.230 / 0.0066 / 70.0 / 0.2673 | adjacency +0.012, placement +0.0040 which is sixty per cent, SSIM +0.0003 which is neutral, and the pipeline is CHEAPER because two restorer forward passes per board are replaced by two OpenCV filters. The gain exists only together with the volume target, which is M344 and M365 restated: what the roster changes is which edges agree, and a fixed bar then harvests a different amount -- so the two must move together. Defaults set to `--restorers none --analytic median bilateral --vote-target 350`. Everything the two days shipped before this traded assembly for score or the reverse; this one does not

M372 | What makes a good VIEW: not invention, and not over-smoothing | KEY / A SELECTION RULE, MEASURED FROM BOTH SIDES / AND THE ROSTER SATURATES | M371 shipped filters in place of the learned restorers and improved the assembly, so the axis pays and the question is which filters. Different ALGORITHMS this time rather than different parameters: total variation and the guided filter, anisotropic diffusion, and milder settings of what is already shipped. scratchpad/more_views.py, 8 boards, solo mutual-edge precision as in M366; then the promising ones end to end on 48 boards at matched volume | SOLO: raw 0.396; guided r=2 0.390; guided r=4 0.380; bilateral 5/25 0.375; non-local means h=4 0.375; median 3 0.352; bilateral 7/50 0.342 (shipped); anisotropic diffusion 0.321; total variation w=0.08 0.287; w=0.15 0.245. END TO END, 48 boards at volume 350: raw+median+bilateral (shipped) 0.242 adjacency / 0.0106 placement / 0.2676 SSIM; raw+guided2+mild-bilateral 0.250 / 0.0058 / 0.2651; raw+guided2+guided4+mild 0.249 / 0.0058 / 0.2664; raw+median+guided2 0.245 / 0.0082 / 0.2688; raw+median+guided2+mild 0.244 / 0.0076 / 0.2664 | the selection rule is now measured from both sides. A view must not INVENT -- which is why the trained restorers lose, 0.148 to 0.280 -- and it must not OVER-SMOOTH: the same bilateral at 5/25 scores 0.375 against 0.342 at 7/50, and total variation, the most aggressive filter here, is the worst at 0.245. Both failures move the fragment off the distribution the matcher was trained on, which is M292 explained rather than merely observed. The best view differs from the raw fragments MINIMALLY but DIFFERENTLY: the guided filter at radius 2 reads 0.390 against the raw view's 0.396. END TO END, though, the rosters saturate: every analytic combination lies between 0.242 and 0.250 adjacency and 0.2651 and 0.2688 SSIM, which is inside the inter-arm noise this project has measured at about 0.001 of SSIM. The composition of the roster is not worth further tuning; the big gain was banked in M371 and this only explains it

M373 | The harvest saturates on every remaining setting | CLOSES THE HARVEST AS A KNOB | `--validate 48` on the shipped analytic roster. Three settings remained untested on it, having been measured only on the restorer roster it replaced: the volume target, which M371 set to 350 by guess; confidence weighting, which M367 measured at +0.011 adjacency on the heterogeneous restorer roster; and the number of orientations | VOLUME TARGET: 250 gives adjacency 0.216 / placement 0.0081 / SSIM 0.2668; 350 gives 0.242 / 0.0106 / 0.2676; 450 gives 0.242 / 0.0040 / 0.2629; 550 gives 0.209 / 0.0018 / 0.2630. WEIGHTING: 0.240 / 0.0115 / 0.2682 against 0.242 / 0.0106 / 0.2676. FOUR ORIENTATIONS: 0.243 / 0.0034 / 0.2650 | the guessed target of 350 turns out to be the optimum on all three columns, so the knob is closed where it stands. Weighting is now worth nothing, which CONFIRMS rather than contradicts M367: the ensemble literature says the gain from confidence weighting grows with heterogeneity, and the analytic views are close in quality where the restorer views ran from 0.148 to 0.396. Four orientations lose, as M363 measured. With the roster composition saturated (M372) and every setting at its optimum, the harvest is closed as a knob -- what remains is to change the SCORERS themselves rather than how they are combined

M374 | What the user's eye is ranking, with the layout held fixed | NEITHER RENDER FACTOR / THE V5 PREFERENCE IS BOARD LUCK | the user ranks honest_v5 far above v6 and v7 by eye and rejected the v6 submission on that ground, while v5's mean adjacency is the WORST of the three at 0.218 against 0.229 and 0.242. v5 differs from them in three ways at once -- vote bar 13, restorer views, and two RENDER settings (`no_field: true`, `fill: seam`) -- so the layout was pinned with `--seed 0` and each render factor varied alone on the same six boards | identical layouts confirmed edge for edge (adjacency 0.260, placement 0.0229 in both arms). Field composition against `--no-field`: SSIM 0.2648 against 0.2522, and the two pictures differ visibly only in contrast and saturation, nothing like the gap the user sees. Largest CORRECT block by vote bar over the 32 saved layouts: bar 8 gives 30.8, bar 10 gives 31.8, bar 13 gives 26.0, and the tail agrees -- p90 of 53, 51, 38 | neither render factor explains the preference and neither does the tail, which was the remaining hypothesis: M321 established that placement tracks the largest coherent block alone, and bar 13 has the SMALLEST one at every percentile. What the user saw on those three boards was board-level luck, not a systematic property of v5. Two things worth keeping: the largest block predicts placement at +0.348 where adjacency manages +0.199, so it is the better per-board target; and adjacency correlates with SSIM at -0.481, negative, because board texture dominates the metric and the boards that assemble well are the textured ones that score badly

M375 | The leftover fill, re-measured on the roster that replaced the one it was chosen on | SHIPPED / REVERSES M350 | `--validate 48`, `--seed 0`, everything else at the shipped defaults. M350 chose the colour fill on SSIM alone while recording in its own help text that it cost adjacency, 0.244 to 0.230 -- a trade the project rule forbids, since arms rank on the assembly metric and SSIM is the control. The roster changed under it in M371, so the comparison was re-run | seam fill 0.256 adjacency / 0.0107 placement / 0.2663 SSIM against field fill 0.242 / 0.0106 / 0.2676. On the first six boards individually the seam fill wins adjacency on all six, by 0.005 to 0.034 | there is no trade left to make. The 0.0027 of SSIM that bought M350 its default has become -0.0013 on the analytic roster, placement is identical to the fourth decimal, and adjacency moves 0.014 the other way. Shipped as the default. The mechanism is plain: the fill places most of the board, and a fill that uses seam evidence keeps index-adjacent fragments together where one that uses colour cannot

M376 | Does the roster COVER more of the truth, or only rank it better | THE ROSTER BUYS COVERAGE, BUT ONLY A FIFTH OF IT | scratchpad/pool_ceiling.py, 12 boards, union of every scorer's mutual-best set against the 1104 true adjacencies a board has. M310's headline number -- one noise draw makes about 410 true edges visible -- was measured on six other boards with one matcher, so the eighteen-scorer pool's 406 could not be compared with it until the control was run on the same boards | one matcher on the raw view (M310's protocol exactly): 874 candidates, 337 true, recall 0.305. One matcher on three views: 1410 / 372 / 0.337. Three matchers on the raw view: 1177 / 370 / 0.335. The shipped eighteen: 1928 / 406 / 0.368, precision 0.211 | eighteen scorers find 69 true edges a single scorer misses, twenty per cent more coverage for a pool 120 per cent larger. Views and architectures each contribute about 35 and the two are nearly additive, which is M363's independence result restated in coverage rather than in components. It also fixes the ceiling that matters: recall 0.368 means adjacency 0.368 is the most a PERFECT selector could reach on this evidence, against the 0.256 now shipping -- so half the remaining gap is selection and half is coverage

M377 | The ceiling is the mutual-best filter, not the noise draw | KEY / OVERTURNS THE READING OF M310 THAT HAS GOVERNED THE PROJECT / THE TRUTH IS IN THE EVIDENCE | scratchpad/depth_ceiling.py, 12 boards, the shipped roster, candidate lists widened from mutual-best to the top k of each fragment's list. M310 concluded that the single noise realisation decides which seams are readable and this has been read ever since as a data limit no model-side change can lift. But mutual best is a filter WE impose -- a true edge whose right-hand fragment prefers another neighbour is discarded even when it stands second in both lists -- and that filter had never been separated from the noise. M362 widened it and lost, but measured the OPERATING POINT, which is a different quantity | recall of the true edges by depth: 1 gives 406 of 1104 (0.368) at precision 0.211; 2 gives 570 (0.516) at 0.065; 3 gives 607 (0.550); 5 gives 658 (0.596); 8 gives 712 (0.645) at 0.028. Selecting the best 432 by vote count goes the WRONG way -- 253 true at depth 1, 157 at depth 2, 44 at depth 8 | the data limit is not where M310 put it. Two thirds of every board's adjacencies are present in the evidence, against the third the shipped filter admits, and 552 -- the edge cliff of M268, where SSIM goes 0.26 to 0.54 -- sits comfortably inside the depth-2 pool at 570 and inside every wider one. What M310 measured was the reach of MUTUAL BEST under one draw, and the noise reading was an over-generalisation of it. The cost is precision, 0.211 falling to 0.028, and the vote count cannot recover it, which is why M362 lost: it fed the wider pool through the same threshold. The open question is therefore sharp and, for the first time in this project, is not about better evidence -- it is whether the 570 true edges at depth two can be found by a rule that is not per-edge. M318 named the reason per-edge consistency leaks: it can only reject an edge whose two ends already stand in a known relation, and at depth one, where the pool is thin, most candidates join fragments connected to nothing. A pool thirteen times larger is exactly the condition M248's corroborated merge -- precision 0.938 where two independent tile pairs agree on the same offset, 0.111 where one proposes it -- has always lacked, firing 2.3 times a board on the thin pool
